England XI when record-breaker Harry Kane made his debut - where are they now?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Harry Kane is set to make even more England history tonight, with the Ballon d’Or winner just one appearance away from overtaking Peter Shilton as his country’s most-capped men’s footballer of all time.

Already the Three Lions’ record goalscorer with 91, Kane will reach 126 caps in the span of 11 years if he is selected by Thomas Tuchel to face Croatia in the Nations League at Wembley on Thursday night.

Kane was handed his senior England debut in a European Championship qualifier against Lithuania back in 2015, his maiden selection after impressing for Tottenham during the 2014/15 season.

Then aged 21, Kane had already grabbed 26 goals in 42 appearances for Spurs by March.

But that was not enough for Kane to be immediately named in the starting lineup by Roy Hodgson, instead coming on a substitute before scoring a debut goal just 79 seconds later.

Ahead of a record night at Wembley, Standard Sport looks at England’s starting lineup when Kane made his England debut.

Long-serving goalkeeper Joe Hart started against Lithuania, as to be expected during what was a period of relative stability between the sticks.

Understudies on the night included Jack Butland and Robert Green, but Hart was established as number one until 2017 - when the emergence of Jordan Pickford prevented him from earning more than 75 caps.

Retired in 2024 after a spell at Celtic and now works as a pundit on the BBC.

Nathaniel Clyne was never first-choice at right-back for Hodgson, with his 14 caps coming across a two-year period.

He managed to outlast Hodgson, but only picked up one more cap under Gareth Southgate. Currently without a club after his second stint at Crystal Palace ended in the summer.

Gary Cahill was enjoying the most consistent spell of his England career in 2015, a trusted centre-back after Rio Ferdinand and John Terry had both retired.

First called up by Fabio Capello, Cahill served under five different England managers in total - his last appearance coming under Southgate at the 2018 World Cup.

Ended his career at Bournemouth and retired in 2022. Now works in the media.

Handed a debut by Hodgson’s predecessor Capello, Phil Jones’ England career was impacted by the same issues as his spell at Manchester United - injuries.

How Harry Kane bucked every trend to become England's superstar

UK braced for first cold snap of season as temperatures set to plummet

Where to watch England vs Croatia: TV channel and live stream

Hodgson liked to include Jones in his team when fit, but he only made 27 appearances for England in total - his last cap coming in the third-place play-off at the 2018 World Cup in Russia.

After spells coaching at United and Blackburn after retiring in 2023, he is now first-team coach at Sheffield United.

Long-serving Everton left-back Leighton Baines had to wait to inherit the same position for the national team from Ashley Cole.

However, the emergence of Luke Shaw meant that the Lithuania game was Baines’ 30th and final cap for England.

He has coached at Everton since retiring in 2020 and currently serves as an assistant to manager David Moyes.

Now a veteran of over 450 Premier League games, 11 years ago Henderson was already established in the England midfield.

A foray in Saudi Arabia briefly halted his England career, but Henderson was still involved in the squad for the 2026 World Cup, making his 90th Three Lions appearance in the group stage game against Panama.

Signed for Chelsea in the summer after leaving Brentford and has become an important member of their midfield under Xabi Alonso, aged 36.

Once a promising Leeds youngster, Fabian Delph was making the fourth of just 20 appearances for England against Lithuania.

Delph’s international career received a brief resurgence under Southgate, used as a makeshift left-back after Pep Guardiola had converted the midfielder into a defender for Manchester City.

Retired in 2022 and has done some media work since then.

11 years ago Michael Carrick was coming to the end of a storied career that included Premier League and Champions League glory at Manchester United.

Already in the post-Sir Alex Ferguson era, United have since slid much further - with Carrick now set to be sacked from his role as head coach at Old Trafford after just one win in 10 Premier League games this term.

Raheem Sterling produced the assist for Kane’s debut England goal, and despite being only 20, the winger had already earned 13 caps.

Since breaking onto the scene as a teenager at Liverpool, Sterling reached new heights at City - before a harsh decline left the 31-year-old hunting for a club after a short stint at Feyenoord in the Netherlands. He has not played for England since 2022.

Like Henderson, veteran Danny Welbeck was signed by Chelsea in the summer to add experience to a young squad at Stamford Bridge.

Unlike his club team-mate, Welbeck has not enjoyed the same longevity with England. In fact, the Lithuania game was his last competitive start for the Three Lions, only earning nine more caps to bring his total tally to 42.

Rather fittingly, Kane replaced Wayne Rooney against Lithuania in the 71st minute - the man he would one day overtake as England’s top goalscorer.

Rooney’s England career never lived up to the expectations in terms of silverware, but his 53 goals in 120 games put ‘Wazza’ into the history books as his country’s top goalscorer and most-capped outfield player - before Kane took both records.

After spells in management at Derby, D.C. United, Birmingham and Plymouth, he is now thriving as a pundit.

Ethan Hawke Reveals What ‘The Lowdown’s Devastating Episode 6 Ending Really Means

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Editor's note: The following contains spoilers for The Lowdown Season 2, Episode 6.

In Episode 6 of Season 2 of the FX series The Lowdown, entitled “Leaving and Coming Back,” Lee Raybon (Ethan Hawke) and his father (Tommy Lee Jones) finally get to a place where they can hash out some unspoken feelings in an attempt to mend old wounds. But since life never turns out the way you expect, the two men lose the opportunity to evolve into something new and Lee is left to process the sudden loss of someone who had a hand in shaping him, for better or worse.

Collider recently got the opportunity to chat with Hawke, who’s also an executive producer on the TV series created by Sterlin Harjo (Reservation Dogs), about Lee’s Season 2 journey. During the interview, he discussed how speaking truth to power sometimes leads to getting punched in the face, how Lee Raybon is like Don Quixote, that final scene in the van with Jones and why the actor’s best scene is yet to come, the relationship with Ginger, and how The Lowdown is just good storytelling.

COLLIDER: I love this show so much. I’m so happy it’s back for a second season, but it feels like Lee Raybon is just as much of a mess, if not more of a mess, this season than he was last season. Do you think he’ll ever stop getting himself into fights and getting beaten up? Because he definitely receives punches much better than he gives them.

ETHAN HAWKE: Yeah, he doesn’t deliver them very well. There’s some kind of larger metaphor at work there about telling truth to power, that when you tell truth to power, you’ve got to get punched in the face, or you have to be willing to. In a way, it’s Lee’s strength that he can absorb all these punches. It’s what he wants to be doing. Do I see a world? Yeah. I’ve seen some great documentaries and things about investigative journalists, and there’s a certain maturity they can get with age where they learn how to fight the fight better. But they’re often willing to put themselves on the line for a good story, and you kind of have to be. I think that’s his strength.

In Season 2, we’re told that we have not seen the best of Lee Raybon, which I don’t doubt. But how can we see the best of someone that can’t seem to get out of his own way? Does he even know what his best is?

HAWKE: That’s where he’s like Don Quixote. He believes that you haven’t seen the best of him because he feels he’s capable of greatness. I hope if we get a chance to keep telling this story, he’s the kind of person who could. Sometimes I think the world is populated with these angels every few counties – people who look after other people. It’s their job in the community and their place there to make sure the powerful are kept in check. I think that Lee is gearing up to hit somebody hard.

Lee Raybon is still allergic to good decisions, which is great news for the best show on TV.

Lee finds himself surrounded by some very powerful people this season. He’s incredibly perceptive when it comes to recognizing corruption, but he’s not always particularly good at recognizing the danger that he’s in at any moment. Is there a part of Lee that actually enjoys provoking people who could destroy him?

HAWKE: I think he loves it. For him, it’s like a punk rocker who jumps into a mosh pit. That’s exactly where he wants to be. He knows the room is rockin’ if it’s really getting crazy.

And he seems to sometimes be surprised by the outcome, which I always find funny.

HAWKE: Maybe this is me projecting this, but I’m a perennial optimist. When things go badly, I’m so shocked, when the writing was clear on the walls to everybody but me, and I think that Lee is like that a little bit. But it doesn’t stop him from continuing to try. There isn’t any screwed up situation that he doesn’t feel he could make right if he just did the perfect thing.

Which makes him fun to watch because it’s more fun to watch him get into trouble than it is for us to actually get into our own trouble.

In Episode 6, the final drive with Lee’s father is devastating, particularly because their last conversation is so ordinary and then suddenly there’s just no possibility of another one. Lee has spent so much time resisting his father’s attempts to reconnect. How did you approach the emotional weight of that scene, especially knowing everything that that Lee loses in that moment?

HAWKE: I just find it’s just one of the best pieces of writing and the way you phrased that question reveals something to me. The moments before the end of Episode 6 are so ordinary, but the possibility for healing is being revealed. And then, once a person dies, the poem is over and the possibility of what might have transpired between these two men is complete. Or maybe it isn’t. As you watch Episodes 7 and 8, I’m really proud of six, seven and eight. They’re my favorites of the series. They’re incredible. What’s that line? “The past doesn’t go anywhere. It’s not even the past.” I’ll tease you with this, Tommy Lee [Jones]’ best scene in the whole series is in Episode 8. It’s phenomenal. And so, the poem isn’t done, but it’s different than you might think.

I love that this show can just let Lee sit in the silence at the end of that episode. What other show could do that?

There’s just something so powerful about him just sitting in the silence in that moment.

HAWKE: Are you talking about when he’s driving the van?

HAWKE: It’s a powerful shot, really beautiful. But that’s what I love about working with (creator) Sterlin [Harjo]. I felt this way watching Reservation Dogs. It’s this weird hybrid between what makes really great TV and what makes really great cinema. He has an original voice. He pauses when you don’t think he’s going to pause. He goes fast when you don’t think he’s going to go fast. He’s irreverent in his storytelling. And working in this classical genre, which is noir, but he’s doing it in his own way. As an audience member, it keeps me on the edge of my seat.

Martha Plimpton talks about joining Season 2 of the FX series 'The Lowdown,' created by Sterlin Harjo and starring Ethan Hawke.

What was the best thing about working with Tommy Lee Jones on that relationship? What did he bring to Lee Sr. that helped define Lee for you in an even deeper way?

HAWKE: Well, he’s so intelligent an actor. The depth of his intelligence radiates. He is a grifter, my father in the show. To be lied to by somebody that smart and that sensitive is really confusing. You can see why Lee has an obsession with telling the truth because he didn’t feel like anybody ever told him the truth. If you’re not sitting on a foundation bedrock of truth, with every step, it’s why we, as a community, need to live in the truth. It’s where the show and what Sterlin is driving it to be about gets into the deep end of the pool. It’s funny and it’s heartfelt and all those good things, but he’s talking about a very serious thing in our lives, about our relationship to the truth.

I loved getting to see the moments of kindness between Lee Sr. and his granddaughter. Obviously, there’s the tension between these two men, but there’s a different chance of him having a relationship with his granddaughter.

HAWKE: Yeah. And we see that in our own lives, don’t we? Just because our relationship with somebody is fraught and complicated and full of faultlines doesn’t mean that their relationship with everybody else is like that. We love this dualistic thinking of, “This person is bad, and this person is good,” as opposed to, “Wow, there’s a lot of good about this person and there’s a lot of bad about this person, and how can I hold both those truths and love this person?” People don’t have to be perfect to be worthy of love.

I love that the intimacy between Lee and Ginger doesn’t arrive through some grand romantic gesture. It’s the quieter, stranger moments of him looking after her, the dish soap in his shower, their time together at the diner. What are these two recognizing in each other that allows them to let their guard down when they’ve spent so much of their lives protecting themselves?

HAWKE: Yeah, that’s a great question. One part of it probably is the impossibility of the situation. Lee’s picking probably the most problematic person for him to fall in love with, to fall in love with. Probably knowing that it can’t work lets his guard come down. We’re so complicated like that as people. But I also think they recognize each other. They’re both a hot mess and they’re both unjudgmental. That’s their superpower, being able to look deeply into somebody. Betty [Gilpin] did a great job of making sure that her character was as multi-dimensional as mine, and that makes the relationship extremely dynamic and real.

Ethan Hawke playing a laid-back investigator in over his head makes for great TV.

I love the show. I loved this season. I love that this is just a show about humanity. There’s something just so special that we can still have that on TV. So, thank you.

HAWKE: It’s our pleasure. That’s exactly what I love about it, too. Even the bad guys have their reasons, and even the good guys have faults. It’s very good storytelling that way.

The Lowdown airs on FX and is available to stream on Hulu.

Access to Shanghai Grand Opera House staircase restricted to paid ticket holders

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Photography by Kaia Wang, Yumeng Zhu, Tian Fangfang, Chen Hao

The Snøhetta-designed Shanghai Grand Opera House has introduced a paid, ticketing system for visitors wanting to access the signature spiral staircase and rooftop viewing platform at the recently opened building in Shanghai.

When Dezeen visited the site, we observed a temporary barricade that had been set up to separate the free-to-access, ground-level public from the spiral staircase leading up to the roof. Access to the staircase and roof now costs 50 yuan (£5.60) per person.

Since opening, access to the architectural feature has been restricted with visitors now required to pre-book tickets online, with the staircase open between 10:30am to 2:30pm.  According to the booking site, people with tickets to see a performance inside the opera house are still required to purchase a ticket and the fee includes accident insurance.

Asked about the apparent change in access requirements, Snøhetta said that demand meant that “visitor management is necessary” and that “access arrangements will continue to develop”.

“As with any major cultural institution, operational procedures continue to evolve during the opening period as the Opera House team gains experience with visitor patterns, capacity needs, and the building’s day-to-day use,” the studio told Dezeen.

“Given the exceptional public interest and the limited capacity of the roof and public stairs, visitor management is necessary to ensure a safe and enjoyable experience for everyone,” it continued.

“Access arrangements will continue to develop with operational experience, but the intention remains the same: to create a building that can be widely enjoyed by the public while balancing accessibility, safety, and the visitor experience.”

Following news that a fee had been introduced, the phrase “is it worth spending 50 yuan to visit Shanghai Grand Opera House” trended on Chinese social media.

Some commenters questioned whether charing to visit the attraction was justifiable, while others sympathised with the need for crowd control. Others shared tips on how to take the best photos of the building without paying the entrance fee.

Visitors Dezeen spoke to at the site were concerned about both the fee and the temporary barriers.

“I don’t think it’s worth the money,” Mr Qiu, who paid to climb the stairs, told Dezeen. “Basically you pay to climb a giant stairs. If you want good photos of the stairs, best to take from afar, which you can do for free,”

Ms Ye, who didn’t pay the entry fee, told Dezeen that she was really disappointed that the barricade was in her photos.

“I don’t know what the point of the barricade is – to separate the paid and unpaid? Or for better crowd circulation? But at least they need to put some effort in designing it to match the beauty of the building,” she said.

“It really is an eye-sore for the photos.”

The building, and its accessible roof, are often compared to Oslo Opera House, which was also designed by Snøhetta and is topped with a walkable roof. According to the studio, both projects were designed to “reduce thresholds wherever possible”.

“Buildings like opera houses inevitably create thresholds through walls, doors, and ticketed programs,” said Snøhetta co-founder Kjetil Thorsen at the time of the Shanghai Grand Opera House opening.

“We see it as our responsibility to challenge those thresholds and make the building as much a civic destination as a performance venue. People should be able to enjoy the building without attending a performance. It should become part of everyday life, a place to spend time, meet others, and experience the city,” he continued.

“A successful landmark is not only recognised by visitors, it is also embraced by the people who live around it and make it part of their everyday lives.”

Several other cultural projects in China have been built with publicly accessible roofs. However, the accessibility presented in concepts has often been restricted when the buildings open.

Also in Shanghai, the Start Museum, designed by French architect Jean Nouvel and opened in 2022, features a public roof that can be used for displaying large-scale art work. However, the space has rarely been used.

The Amateur Architecture Studio-designed Fuchun Cultural Museum Complex is defined by a continuous undulating roof to resemble the experience of climbing the local Fuchun Mountain. However, the roof didn’t open to the public for four years and is now only open to public visits for one hour a day, three days a week.

Photography by Kaia Wang, Yumeng Zhu, Tian Fangfang, Chen Hao

5 new mobile games to try this week - November 12th, 2026

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Fresh mobile picks you should give a go this week

Who doesn't love new games? We certainly do - there's just something about the exhilaration of a new title you can't wait to dive into, as the loading screen pops up and you enter a brave new world.

Of course, new mobile games are a dime a dozen these days, and who has the time to scour the iPhone, iPad and/or Android stores to catch the latest title that drops? Fret not - we've gathered a quick list of 5 new mobile games you should try this week, whether that's an undiscovered indie gem or a high-profile AAA masterpiece.

That said, you can head on over to our NEW WEEKLY MOBILE GAMES hub if you're on the hunt for more curated adventures to obsess over!

Perhaps the release that a good number of people on our team are the most excited about is Warhammer Survivors, and despite not being a Warhammer fan myself, I can totally understand why. This Survivors-like take on the popular franchise is, as Iwan and Will put it, the perfect pair, especially since it was crafted in partnership with poncle of Vampire Survivors fame.

You'll try to survive in this "bullet heaven" shooter using more than 40 characters from Warhammer 40,000 and Warhammer Age of Sigmar, taking down endless hordes of foes across mobile-optimised controls to boot.

One of the most important elements of a classic whodunit is to piece together clues based on what suspects are saying - but what if you had to solve the case across an entirely wordless affair? I suppose that's the biggest challenge that Hillthorn poses to anyone who dares to take it on, as you'll have to put two and two together to get to the bottom of things using purely card-based clues.

Time is of the essence, so you'll really need to put your detective cap on to find a child who's gone missing. There are multiple endings to discover, so it's safe to say that after every short run, you'll likely want to revisit the eerie mysteries of Hillthorn over and over again.

I don't think there's anything else that embodies the pick-up-and-play nature of mobile more perfectly than one-thumb portrait-mode endeavours, and Rocket Rally is right up that alley! It is, as you might expect, a quick arcade racer where you'll need to boost and drift to the finish line in just 30 seconds, and with a wide variety of rally cars you can add to your collection too.

As you progress, you'll unlock upgrades for your car to ramp up your races, all so you can beat your own best time or aim for the top spot in the leaderboards.

I feel like Crossy Road Rush is perfectly suited for Apple Arcade, as the absence of ads and in-app purchases definitely adds to the endless runner's charm - and especially so since Crossy Road is such a staple on mobile! This time around, you'll be running through colourful environments similar to Subway Surfers, with a whole bunch of cool stuff to collect and obstacles to overcome.

There are more than 50 levels to discover and daily challenges to take on, as well as family sessions you can join in on with up to four players. I'd say it's definitely a fresh take on the franchise - after all, where else can you squad up with your fam to take on a giant Octoboss?

The concept of climbing an endless tower is nothing new, but I suppose this is the first time I've ever encountered one where you're meant to crank it up - quite literally. A Fool's Errand was originally made for the Playdate, where you turn a literal crank to help your hero ascend. Now that it's out on mobile, you'll still do the same climbing without the physical crank - but with the same atmospheric visuals and intriguing story.

There are plenty of traps to avoid along the way, and it's really all about the journey as you keep climbing on - as the title suggests - a fool's errand. Whether or not what awaits you at the top is worth it is anybody's guess - and I suppose you'll just have to make the climb yourself to find out.

Fallout Officially Returns With New Deathclaw Expansion

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Devin Coull is a Toronto-based writer, producer, and on-camera host. He spent over six years working in gaming and esports, most recently at theScore Esports, created content for millions of viewers around the world.

Fallout 76 players have a two-word reason to log back in: Pet Deathclaws. That's right, players on Xbox Series X|S and PlayStation 5 can now experience traveling through Appalachia alongside one of the series' most iconic creatures in the game's Public Test Server (PTS), ahead of the World Pets update officially hitting live servers on December 1.

Fallout 76 didn't exactly get off to the best start. But credit to Bethesda, the studio has been fairly consistent about updating the game with gameplay fixes and new content. Not every update has been a hit, but this upcoming release gives fans another reason to hop back into the Wasteland while we wait for information on the Fallout 3 and Fallout New Vegas remakes. Pets were previously limited to just chilling around your C.A.M.P., but now you can take any pet you own out into the world as a companion by simply summoning them from your Pip-Boy. And since this is the Public Test Server, Bethesda has kindly given players access to one pet of each species to try out. However, keep in mind that once the test ends and the update hits live servers, you'll only be able to keep the pets you've previously obtained.

Like companions from the mainline entries in the Fallout series, players will be able to command their pets to stay, follow, attack enemies, and protect them from harm. Pets can also have their HP and damage increased through leveling up and combat upgrades, with progression eventually reaching a level cap of 200. As for which pets you'll be able to take on your adventures, there are cats, dogs, Radhogs, and, of course, the aforementioned Deathclaws. Deathclaws have long been some of the most dangerous creatures in the Fallout universe. I'll personally never forget the first time I ventured into Quarry Junction a little too early and got torn to shreds. Having one of these apex predators as a friend this time around is quite the reversal and a welcome power fantasy.

Deathclaws also come with two unique passive abilities. Dark Titan lets them find Black Titanium while exploring, while Fun-Festation grants extra Legendary Items for completing Infestations. The latter can eventually reward three-star items as it levels up. Other pet species also offer their own buffs, which are detailed in Bethesda's PTS announcement. The PTS also includes an update to Skyline Valley, which first appeared in the June 2024 update of the same name. The region is getting some much-needed TLC, with reworked mountains and hillsides, new pathways, and additional vegetation. Players can also pick up bounty hunting quests and start a new collectible questline involving mysterious glass canisters.

Finally, players can get an early look at Hideouts, large-scale enemy outposts that offer rewards for clearing them. Right now, the PTS features two locations: The Forever Farm in Burning Springs and Vault 63's Immortality Sector in Skyline Valley. Each has four difficulty levels, with tougher challenges offering better rewards. Hideouts are still in early testing, however, and aren't expected to fully launch until spring 2027. Any rewards earned during the test are temporary.

To try out the new content, install the Fallout 76 Public Test Server through your Xbox or PlayStation game library. You'll also need to join the Bethesda Game Studios Discord server and follow the instructions to obtain an access code. Once again, the World Pets update is currently planned for December 1, while Hideouts are still a ways off.

Young duo at heart of Nations League's meanest defence

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Northern Ireland play Ukraine in Belfast on Saturday before travelling to Budapest to face Hungary on Tuesday

Across his two spells as Northern Ireland boss, Michael O'Neill has been blessed with no shortage of centre-back talent.

Jonny Evans, Aaron Hughes, Gareth McAuley and Craig Cathcart were mainstays of his first stint in charge - so much so that, in some of the biggest games of O'Neill's managerial career at Euro 2016, the quartet all started across a flat back-four.

Since returning to the job four years ago, O'Neill has switched to three at the back, with Sunderland duo Trai Hume and Dan Ballard providing an injection of Premier League quality in what remains a side largely drawn from the Championship and League One.

But with Hume, something of a Swiss army knife in the squad, covering the injured Conor Bradley on the right flank and even starting in centre midfield against Ukraine last month, and Ballard presently nursing an injured hamstring, change has been required through this Nations League campaign.

Despite those forced alterations in defence, the side go into their concluding Group B2 fixtures against Ukraine and Hungary top of their pool and the only side at any level of the competition yet to concede a goal.

At the heart of their defensive efforts have been Ruairi McConville and Tom Atcheson, two young centre-backs already described by their international manager as having "no ceiling".

Birthdays and broken lamps - at home with the Charles brothers

NI can draw on strong club form in key window

At 21-years-old, Norwich City's McConville is the more senior of the pair.

Having been given his debut off the bench at home to Belarus two years ago this week, among the former Brighton man's early international assignments was trying to keep a rampant Alexander Isak in check during a 5-1 defeat by Sweden.

In the short time since he has looked an increasingly commanding figure, leading this Nations League group in clearances made with both head and boot.

"It would be unbelievable to keep that record for these last two games and go the whole of the campaign," he said of what is Northern Ireland's longest run without conceding a goal since 2017.

McConville notes that the streak is a team effort and product of collective work-rate, rather than just the defensive three alone.

"We're a team that works hard, a team that doesn't want to give anything up.

"We're confident in ourselves and we know that we're a good side."

It is McConville who has taken on the Ballard role in the middle of the defence for the past three games, with Atcheson on the right and Oxford United's Ciaron Brown, himself superb through the campaign so far, playing from the left.

On the developing understanding between an emerging unit, McConville added: "We're enjoying playing together.

"It's still early days together and we have to keep taking it game by game and doing what the gaffer tells us and the work on the training pitch.

"We don't have much time together when we're here [in international camp] so it's important to do all we can."

Watch on BBC iPlayer and BBC Two NI from19:15 GMT; Listen on BBC Sounds and BBC Radio Ulster; Watch, listen, live text and clips on BBC Sport app & website

Atcheson's comfort on the ball has been an asset across his six caps

After 16 caps, McConville is a relative veteran compared to Atcheson, who made his international bow in a friendly against Wales in March at a time when he had only four senior club starts to his name at Blackburn Rovers.

The most recent of those had come under O'Neill, then in a short stint as caretaker manager at the Championship club.

Clearly impressed by what he saw from the now 20-year-old at Ewood Park, Atcheson made his first Northern Ireland start against Guinea in June, a game in which he scored the winner with a fabulous flick before later being shown a red card.

Some 428 minutes into his international career, Atcheson is still to experience what it feels like to concede a goal.

The England-born defender feels Ballard "is definitely a big miss" to the side, but believes the squad's understanding of O'Neill's desired system is an asset to those who have starred during the Sunderland man's latest absence.

"I feel like everyone knows what they need to do, everyone knows their job, the details of the formation we play and system," he said.

"That's so key and I think that's why we're so solid defensively.

"It's a team thing, we're set up right, we're very on it all the time and everyone gives their all."

Star Trek Officially Restores Seven Of Nine's Humanity In Unimatrix Zero

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Seven of Nine's journey to reclaim her humanity is one of Star Trek: Voyager's most compelling character arcs, and no moment in the series hits quite as hard as the two-part episode "Unimatrix Zero." Although Seven had already been liberated from the Borg Collective, the episode introduced a mysterious sort of virtual sanctuary where certain drones could temporarily escape the hive mind while they regenerated.

Within this hidden world, Seven appeared as Annika Hansen, her original human identity, completely free of the Borg implants that had come to define her appearance. It was an incredible moment for the character, offering a glimpse of the life that had been stolen from her when she was assimilated as a child.

The episode also revealed a forgotten romance from Seven's time in the Collective, further complicating her relationship with her past. Now, more than 25 years later, Star Trek is officially revisiting this pivotal moment in Seven of Nine's story with a brand-new release paying homage to her lost humanity.

Star Trek: Voyager fans are getting an incredible tribute to one of Seven of Nine's most iconic storylines in the upcoming Unimatrix Zero adaptation. The new MTG scene cards recreate the pivotal two-part episode, including Seven's appearance as Annika Hansen, completely free of her Borg implants.

For those unfamiliar, scene cards are special borderless Magic cards featuring interconnected artwork in a grid-style setup. When arranged together, they form a larger mosaic depicting an iconic franchise moment. The Unimatrix Zero scene, illustrated by Gal Or, features six cards arranged vertically, 2-by-3. The full set includes the following cards:

The full art captures both the threatening Borg Collective and Janeway's efforts to liberate the drones, with Seven's original human form being a great callback for longtime Voyager fans. The six cards are available in non-foil and traditional foil versions through Play Boosters and Collector Boosters. You can take a look at the full mosaic here:

Unimatrix Zero is just one of several incredible scenes included in the crossover. Other Booster scenes include Deep Space Find, which has characters from Deep Space Nine; Mirror, Mirror, which revisits the titular Original Series episode; and Showdown at Sector 001, recreating Picard's assimilation as Locutus. Two additional Scene Boxes, Divergent Timeline and Enterprise-Q, celebrate the Kelvin timeline and Q's memorable encounters with the Enterprise crew, respectively.

With the crossover officially launching on November 13, 2026, anticipation is building among Star Trek fans, particularly collectors eager to get their hands on these stunning tributes to the franchise's history.

Beyond the scene cards, the larger release features Play Boosters, Collector Boosters, specialty bundles, and four dedicated 100-card Commander decks: Federation Fleet, Landing Party, Klingon Fury, and We Are the Borg. These decks spotlight Picard, Spock, Worf, and the Borg Queen, respectively, while bringing numerous familiar characters and starships into Magic's gameplay as well.

Unfortunately, some fans may have to wait a little longer for certain products, as Wizards of the Coast has confirmed production and shipping delays affecting several regions, including North America. Scene Boxes and Bundles are arriving later than originally planned, while Commander decks may miss the November 13 launch.

Draft Night and Beam Me Up Bundles have reportedly also been delayed beyond release day, although Play Boosters remain on schedule. Despite these setbacks, Star Trek and Magic fans are in for an exciting weekend with the official launch on Friday.

Bob Dylan’s Meeting With Pope Leo: People Fixated on the Hood But the Hands Spoke Volumes

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Timothée Chalamet fever aside, nothing Bob Dylan has done in the wild in recent years has been so widely discussed by mainstream media — and so instantly meme-ified — as the photos of Dylan, in a hood pulled up and over his head, meeting Pope Leo XIV at the Vatican on Nov. 4.

That one wore black, the other white had Twitter tripping over itself to make pithy comments like it was a New Yorker cartoon caption contest. And Dylan’s hoodie was instant headline fodder. The Wall Street Journal clutched their pearls. GQ announced that Dylan dressed like Chalamet. The Daily Beast reported outrage! Star Wars was mentioned… a lot.

But Antonio Spadaro, a Jesuit priest serving as undersecretary for culture and education at the Holy See who brokered the meeting, says Dylan’s attire spoke so loudly that people missed the larger message.

“People talked a lot about what Dylan was wearing that afternoon and very little about what the pope had written,” Spadaro says, referring to the fact that the pope quoted “Go Away You Bomb” — an unpublished 1963 Dylan work — in the preface of his 2026 book, Disarmed and Disarming: Peace Is a Gift.

In the work, Dylan wrote to the bomb: “I hate you cause yer man-made and man-owned, an’ man-handled.”

“In 1963, a young man just past 20, in New York, told a bomb to its face that it was the work of human hands,” Spadaro says. Sixty-three years later “the author of that sheet of paper — which no one had wanted to buy at auction — shook hands with his most authoritative reader. For anyone who believes in the weight of words, it is a small parable. The hood, by comparison, is a side note.”

Since the musical and papal summit, Spadaro — a native of Messina, Italy, and former editor-in-chief of La Civiltà Cattolica, who now writes a Substack —  has been doing the Lord’s work by sharing photos of the Midwesterners’ meeting on social media.

He posted a few shots of their handshake. One photo, which he posted twice, was striking: A zoomed-in shot of Dylan’s hand clasping the pope’s.  Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

With its blue veins, purple-ish hue, and dry white powdery skin, Dylan’s hand looking every minute of his 85 years. But the appendage was also a peek behind the curtain. A lift under the hood. That papery hand seemed to say: “I’m human and I need this spiritual touch as much as the next guy.”

We asked Spadaro, 60, to elaborate on the photos he posted on Instagram. Via email, the priest and author —  whose most recent books are A passo d’uomo with a foreword by Patti Smith, and Conversations on Faith written with Martin Scorsese — shared his thoughts on hands, hoods, and how David Sedaris fits into all this.

I was so moved by the photo you posted on Instagram, a close-up of their hands clasped. Almost like a Renaissance painting detail, a meeting of two old sages. What prompted you to zoom in that close?The hands said what the hood kept hidden. Bob’s face was in shadow; his hands, though, were bare, and he had bared them himself, taking off his gloves to greet the pope. That handclasp holds the hand of the young man, just past 20, who typed out “Go Away You Bomb” in 1963, and the hand of the pope who quoted it against nuclear rearmament 63 years later.

You’re right to think of painting. I thought of Dürer’s “Praying Hands,” the 1508 study in which the veins and the worn skin tell an entire life.

By tightening the frame, I took away the fame and the colors of their clothes. What remained were two men well along in years, clasping hands, and it seemed to me the truest photograph of that afternoon. Related Content Hear Bob Dylan Play ‘Love and Theft’ Classic ‘Sugar Baby’ for First Time Since 2012 A Jesuit Priest Explains How He Facilitated Bob Dylan’s Meeting With the Pope Bob Dylan Meets With Pope Leo XIV in a Private Audience All the Albums Coming Out in November

Dylan morphs over the years, sartorially. When he was in his Nudie-suit-and-cowboy-hat-phase, he wore that to meet Pope John Paul II. I love that Dylan stayed true to his current phase here. Did the pope have a reaction to that? Did anyone?One detail about 1997 makes the story even better. In Bologna, Dylan performed in his cowboy hat, but when he climbed the steps toward John Paul II, he took it off. This time the hood stayed where it was, and what came off were the gloves, so he could shake the pope’s hand. In 1997 he bared his head; in 2026, his hands.

The only one who hesitated, I admit, was me. At St. Anne’s Gate, I asked his team whether it might be better to lower it, and they explained that the hood, these days, is part of how Bob moves through the world, onstage and off. That was enough for me.

Leo looked the man in the eye, and the hood receded into the background. On social media, some people brought up Emperor Palpatine [from Star Wars]. The scene was much simpler. The pope was perfectly at ease, as if he already knew Bob would show up that way.

What sparked this meeting? Did Dylan want to meet the pope or vice versa? How did you get involved?The wish was there on both sides: Bob wanted to meet the first American pope, and the pope needed no convincing. A man who has already quoted you in a footnote is ready to receive you.

I knew the right people on both sides, and I found myself in the middle, as a kind of hub. The real work was scheduling. Dylan played Milan on Monday and was due to play Rome Thursday. The pope was leaving for Latin America on Friday. That left Wednesday, a day when Bob had no show. That was all it took.

The pope seems to be a Dylan fan. He quoted from “Go Away You Bomb” in his book —that’s a deep pull. I’m a Dylan nerd, and had to Google it. I could only find 2013 articles about those lyrics going up for auction at Christie’s. They never even sold. Is the Pope a hardcore Dylanologist?It depends on what we mean by Dylanologist. The word makes me think of A.J. Weberman, who in the early 1970s went through Dylan’s trash in Greenwich Village. In that sense, I’d say definitely not.

[That] you had to Google, though, says a lot. “Go Away You Bomb” isn’t on any record. Dylan wrote it in 1963 and never recorded it. It was meant for a songbook against the atomic bomb that Izzy Young, of the Folklore Center, never managed to publish.

The sheet stayed among Young’s papers for half a century, then went up for auction at Christie’s — that’s how the lyrics became public. The pope came to it as a reader, so much so that in the footnote he calls it a “poetic composition” and places it next to Augustine and Paul VI, with title and year.

He could have chosen “Masters of War,” which everyone knows. I don’t see a Dylanologist so much as a reader who knows where to look.

You wrote about Jim Musselman, the pope’s Villanova classmate, on Instagram. Tell me more about their Dylan connection. The two met at Villanova in the 1970s, in a philosophy class, and immediately found common ground in two things, baseball and Bob Dylan.

After Leo’s election, Musselman recalled that the two of them talked about music a lot, and that he had given his friend an essay arguing that Dylan was the great poetic talent of their generation. Which essay, I don’t know.

I really like the rest of his story. Musselman founded a folk label, Appleseed Recordings, and for years released records by Pete Seeger and other singers of social conscience. Leo’s Dylan comes from there: from a classmate with a passion for songs that set out to change things.

Dylan had a private talk with the pope. You said you weren’t there.No, and I think that’s as it should be. I understood that Bob wanted to be alone with Leo, and we left the room. I saw the before and the after.

Before: two men sitting across from each other at a table in the library, some light banter, the exchange of gifts, a rosary on one side and a signed copy of Chronicles on the other.

After: Bob walking out in silence, deeply absorbed, with the look of a man who is happy and has no intention of explaining why. Then again, Dylan has spent 60 years not explaining his songs. He certainly wasn’t going to start with that conversation.

Dylan signed your copy of 64 Lyrics. I love that he used your four-color BIC pen. People jumped on that detail on social media.“64 Lyrics” is a bilingual anthology published in Italy by Crocetti in its poetry series: 64 songs — English on one side, the Italian translation on the other. It was edited by Alessandro Carrera, Dylan’s Italian translator, and Carlo Feltrinelli, his Italian publisher, who was with us that afternoon in the Apostolic Palace.

At some point in the afternoon I asked him if he would sign it for me. He smiled and said “maybe,” and I pulled the pen out of my pocket. I love those pens. I clicked down the black myself.

I understand why people had fun with it. On an afternoon of loggias and gendarmes, the object everyone remembers is a pen that costs pocket change. To me it seems perfect: even a Nobel Laureate, when the moment comes, signs with whatever pen is at hand.

You also connected Patti Smith’s meeting with Pope Leo this past summer.  Patti and I have known each other for five years. We met in Amsterdam, at a debate held by the Nexus Institute, which had invited us both; then I interviewed her in New York, and a friendship grew out of that, made mostly of long breakfasts near her home. When I’m in town we get together.

After the election of Leo, at some point it became clear to me that she wanted to meet him. In late July, Patti was touring Europe and was due to play Rome. That was the opportunity. The pope said yes, and I made two schedules fit together.

What was that like to witness? Again, I love the post you shared on Instagram — she’s gazing at the pope, almost like a kid. I saw a woman who has sung in front of enormous crowds become almost speechless. [Her] first sound was “Hi,” and anyone who knows “Wave” knows where that comes from. In that 1979 song, Patti imagines talking to John Paul I and begins exactly that way. Trending Stories Sade Are Not Attending Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Induction Ceremony The ‘You Can See Everything’ Interview You’ve Been Waiting For Megan Thee Stallion and Daniel Radcliffe Reunite for Season Two of ‘Reggie Dinkins’ Judge Sets Steven Tyler Sex Assault Trial Date

Forty-seven years later, the imagined conversation became real. Leo caught the reference right away. They talked for about 40 minutes, and at the end she took her leave with “Bye. Bye.” Between those two tiny words was a conversation between two people from Chicago who recognized each other.

I interviewed David Sedaris about meeting the pope. Afterward, he visited the papal tailor, Gammarelli. He told me he still wears the cassock. He went with Stephen Colbert and a slew of comedians — Whoopi Goldberg, Conan O’Brien, Julia Louis-Dreyfus. Were you there?I was there, and I had a great time. That meeting was an initiative of the Dicastery for Culture and Education together with the Dicastery for Communication. If you think about it, it’s a nice counterpoint to the Dylan story: the humorist left the Vatican in search of a cassock, and the singer-songwriter came in wearing a hood.

Why Henry Cavill & Mike Flanagan’s Sci-Fi Prime Video Series Is Taking So Long

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Henry Cavill and Mike Flanagan's Prime Video sci-fi series just got another key update.

Cavill has provided an update on him and Flanagan's upcoming adaptation of Warhammer 40,000, revealing why it's taking so long to make. Cavill's Warhammer 40,000 TV show was reported to be in development in December 2022, with the actor set to star in and executive produce the series. The series has been in development ever since, with Flanagan joining the project in July 2026 following a deal he made with Prime Video. However, almost four years later, the show remains in development for the streaming platform.

Speaking with Inverse about his forthcoming projects, Cavill opened up about the progress he and Flanagan are making on their Warhammer 40,000 TV series. He explained the core reason behind the series taking so long is that he wants to ensure that it's "absolutely perfect" before it's ready to begin production. He says faithfulness to the source material is key to its development, while praising Flanagan as a fantastic creative partner in bringing the grim sci-fi series to a new medium.

It’s going really well. Obviously near and dear to my heart, Warhammer. So it’s one of those things which I want to make sure is absolutely perfect, and we’re protecting the IP whilst also telling the best story we can possibly tell, which will lead to better stories. Mike Flanagan is an incredible partner. He’s such an enormously talented man.

Cavill's update comes weeks after Flanagan revealed Warhammer 40,000 finally has a starting point, with the two creatives having determined the best way to approach the live-action TV series. As Cavill's statement expresses, the series will be the starting point of a shared universe produced under Amazon, which will see the lore of the tabletop game expanded into high-budget productions. But, given the intricacies of the universe and the passion behind it, getting it right is key to its success.

It's one of many large projects in the Warhammer 40,000 universe currently in development. Another is the real-time strategy game Total War: Warhammer 40,000, a video game that allows players to control battalions of troops across various planets within the larger lore. There's also forthcoming RPG Warhammer 40,000: Dark Heresy, which arrives January 28, 2027, and a third installment in the universe's highly-popular Space Marine series. There is no shortage of Warhammer 40,000 projects to come.

But what makes Cavill and Flanagan's so important is that it will be the first released on a broad TV scale. The pair are developing the world in a way that reflects uncharted, experimental territory for the franchise. Finding a starting point was the most important part, though, meaning development is clearly continuing in a positive direction. And, with Cavill wanting to ensure the franchise's TV debut is as powerful as possible, it makes sense for him and Flanagan to take their time.

With the show now having a starting point, though, it seems like there will be more positive news about the Warhammer 40,000 TV show once concrete details are secured. For now, the ambitious project appears to be heading in the right direction, which means good things for when the show finally does premiere on Prime Video.

Palmetto Publishing Releases Dr. Paul J. Carr's History of Riverhead's Suffolk County Fairs

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

The newly published volume chronicles the Suffolk County Agricultural Society's annual exhibitions from 1818 through 1941 and the farming economy of eastern Long Island that surrounded them.

CHARLESTON, S.C., Nov. 11, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Palmetto Publishing has released Farming, Community, and the Legacy of The Suffolk County Agricultural Society's Fairs, a local history by Dr. Paul J. Carr, written with Riverhead Town Historian Georgette Lane Case. The book documents the agricultural fairs held at Riverhead, New York, and the farming communities of the East End of Long Island that built them.

The Suffolk County Agricultural Society was founded in 1818 and held its first cattle show in Smithtown. For seventy-six years its annual exhibition took place in Riverhead, where growers competed for premiums over pumpkins, potatoes, cauliflower, cattle, and poultry. Balloonists ascended over the grandstand. Jockeys complained the track was too narrow. Semi-pro ballplayers drew betting crowds, early aviators flew above upturned faces, and President Theodore Roosevelt rode onto the grounds on horseback to address the gathering.

Carr sets the fairs down year by year, from 1865 to 1941, then follows the attempted revivals that came after. The fair buildings were auctioned in 1945 and a school rose on the ground. Around that chronology runs a second account: how land once dismissed as waste was proved fertile, how the railroad carried cauliflower to the city and politicians to the midway, and how potatoes, sod, oysters, and later vineyards reshaped the North Fork. The book also tests several durable local legends against the record.

Dr. Paul J. Carr stated, "I walked into the Riverhead Town Historian's office in 2020 expecting to catalogue documents, and Georgette mentioned she had always hoped to write a pamphlet about the fairs. The research outgrew a pamphlet almost immediately. No full account existed, and it seemed wrong to leave it that way."

The research draws on newspaper accounts, premium lists, treasurers' reports, and the holdings of the Riverhead Town Historian's office. The narrative moves through the post Civil War years, the Roaring Twenties, the Great Depression, the Long Island Express Hurricane of 1938, and the approach of the Second World War. The book is intended for genealogists, local historians, students, and general readers researching Long Island families, farms, businesses, and landmarks. It sits alongside county histories and historical society journals, and arrives as farmland preservation and the North Fork wine economy continue to draw attention to the region's agricultural past.

Farming, Community, and the Legacy of The Suffolk County Agricultural Society's Fairs is available for purchase online at Amazon.com and BarnesandNoble.com. For more information about the author, please visit any of their social media platforms.

Website: https://riverheadfairhistory.com/

About the Author: In 2020, Dr. Paul J. Carr walked into the Riverhead Town Historian's office expecting to catalogue documents. Georgette Lane Case, the town's historian, mentioned she had long hoped to write a pamphlet on the Suffolk County Agricultural Society's fairs. The research that followed, covering the Riverhead fairs from 1865 to 1941 and the farming that surrounded them, outgrew a pamphlet almost immediately and became Farming, Community, and the Legacy of The Suffolk County Agricultural Society's Fairs.

Carr's interest in history accumulated over many years rather than arriving all at once. He has been an avid reader of history, pursued graduate studies in the subject, taught social studies, served as an archivist at the Long Island Rail Road Museum, and volunteered at the Riverhead Historian's office. That combination shows in the book's method: treasurer's reports and premium lists sit beside balloon ascensions, diving horses, and the occasional runaway sulky. He describes the project as a celebration and a dedication to the East End's farming ancestors.

Georgette Lane Case has served as Historian for the Town of Riverhead, New York, since 2001. She works to preserve and promote the town's rich and diverse heritage, and serves as a research resource for genealogists, historians, students, and the public, fielding questions about local families, businesses, landmarks, and community history. Her documentation of Riverhead's past is aimed squarely at the future, at ensuring that later generations understand how their community came to look the way it does.

Title: Farming, Community, and the Legacy of The Suffolk County Agricultural Society's FairsAuthor: Dr. Paul J. Carr With Georgette Lane Case, Riverhead Town Historian

10 Most Creative Traversal Abilities in Metroidvanias

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Ronald is a video game journalist with more than 10 years of experience in the industry, working on several of the most important Spanish websites.

Movement abilities in Metroidvanias are a fundamental part of their appeal, determining both the pace of exploration and our ability to interact with the surrounding world.

The genre is built on understanding spaces, their composition, and how to traverse them, making movement a crucial variable regardless of the game's perspective, mechanics, or philosophy.

As a result, the possibilities a game enables for movement along the Cartesian plane largely determine how satisfying, tense, or rewarding a campaign will be, depending on its intended purpose.

Therefore, in order to pay homage to those video games that make the best possible use of the philosophy of moving from one place to another, here are the ten most creative traversal abilities in Metroidvanias.

One of the main problems games in this genre face when creating spectacular movement abilities lies in their limitations, as they have to be balanced so that they aren't too powerful.

For this reason, Silk Soar from Hollow Knight: Silksong is a masterpiece of design. Among all the powers in Metroidvanias that let you fly upwards, it's part of the few that properly contextualize the limits of its capabilities.

Not only do you acquire it in the game's last third, unexpectedly opening paths out of nowhere, but its use is also tied to your needle, requiring the presence of a ceiling to be used, so you can't just break the map's traversal in its entirety.

In terms of creativity, it's on the spectrum's simpler side, yet it solves a major verticality imbalance by granting you a great deal of ease in exploration without making you overpowered, which is a tremendous feat.

Going in the opposite direction from the previous entry, Pipistrello and the Cursed Yoyo is a constant display of reinterpreting Metroidvanias' most common forms of movement, with Walk the Dog being the crown jewel.

The game plays with yo-yo tricks to establish its mechanics, adapting them with thematic coherence to make you feel that, more than just having powers within a video game, you're learning new maneuvers to show off.

In this particular instance, it's as simple as being able to move orthogonally across the map at a greater speed, but this is maximized thanks to the bounce pads distributed throughout all areas that change your direction and make the ability the key to numerous puzzles.

Pipistrello and the Cursed Yoyo is brimming with ideas magnificently adapted to its setting, making it by far one of the most inventive Metroidvanias to come out this decade.

Well Dweller breaks one of the genre's cardinal rules by not including a double jump at any point in its story, though it compensates with a Slingshot mechanic that is simply mesmerizing.

The title allows you to throw your weapon and embed it in any surface to use it as a springboard and jump higher than usual, encouraging inquisitive and creative exploration in a truly compelling way.

Most of the game's movement is based on the biomes you find yourself in, whether in the form of bubbles, air currents, or elevators, and it works precisely because this direct yet versatile ability adapts to any situation.

Of course, it leaves you without any means of defense, but, like Kratos's Leviathan Axe, Well Dweller's matchstick flies back to you when you need it, which also leads to spectacular and almost cinematic moments when transitioning from platforming to combat.

If you're looking for games that subvert your expectations, try these eight Metroidvanias that totally reinvent themselves halfway through.

In terms of inventiveness, Animal Well is a couple of notches above the rest of the genre, with all its progression tools being as unique as they're rewarding.

Since the title is more puzzle-based than platforming-focused, most of its abilities are proportional to said distribution, but the Disc is a versatile component that you can use both to solve environmental puzzles and to traverse in seemingly impossible ways.

Throwing the frisbee and timing its trajectory to hop on and soar through the air never gets old, both because of how mechanically satisfying it is and because of all the paths mastering this skill opens up.

Animal Well is a sandbox where most of its castles are built through iteration and experimentation, but fortunately, you have the Disc on your side to make the journey faster, smoother, and more enjoyable.

Despite my reservations about Constance due to its short length and limited side content, its movement mechanics are some of the smoothest I've ever seen in the genre, with Paint Dive being the standout.

It functions as both a dash and a dematerialization of the character, allowing her to blend into the environment and pass through obstacles and hazards as a graceful mass of purple paint that seamlessly integrates with the surroundings.

In its abstract form, it's simply a directional boost, yet its execution is elevated by the environments, which force you to use the ability in increasingly diverse ways, ensuring it's more than just a regular push.

Given that platforming is Constance's forte, Paint Dive plays a prominent role throughout the entire campaign, making it a proportionally more important element of the game than many of the other abilities on this list.

From classic Resident Evil to Dark Souls, these are eight Non-Metroidvanias that heavily influenced the genre.

Returning to what I mentioned about Silk Soar and the problems of having very powerful abilities, the Crystal Arrows in Aeterna Noctis take the conversation to another level by granting you the power to teleport to wherever you aim them.

Certainly, they're used in many of the most excruciatingly difficult platforming sections the genre has ever seen, but also in one of the deepest movement systems.

Being able to chain together consecutive teleportations while fighting and traversing Aeterna's world quickly becomes a delight, especially once you get used to them and feel you've mastered their immeasurable offensive and defensive potential.

While Aeterna Noctis' structure doesn't fully capitalize on the Crystal Arrows, they remain magnificent and satisfying, surprisingly without ever breaking the game's sequence.

Castlevania: Symphony of the Night dares to take its most unusual ability and only make it necessary for a limited number of sections, yet the Mist Form remains an unforgettable memory for anyone who plays the game.

Becoming an incorporeal entity to pass through bars is the kind of power fantasy that only a character like Alucard can materialize, with the added significance of having achieved something so astonishing thirty years ago.

Although there aren't many mandatory instances where you must use it, the ability is a major surprise when you unlock it, primarily when it becomes an incredibly powerful toxic weapon that allows you to both move freely and eliminate all enemies in your path.

Years have passed, and I don't think I've ever been so impacted by unlocking a new ability, which only speaks to Castlevania: Symphony of the Night's timeless greatness.

Of all the innovations that Ori and the Blind Forest brought to the genre during the formative years when its heirs were still establishing themselves, Bash is undoubtedly the most prominent.

The ability to bounce off both enemies and projectiles to move omnidirectionally and reach new heights and locations is absurdly incredible from the very first minute to the last.

The mechanic was significantly improved in the sequel, yet its stellar and unprecedented appearance in the franchise's first title is a core memory of the genre, particularly due to its use in combat to stylize every fight sequence.

I must admit Ori and the Blind Forest isn't among my favorite Metroidvanias, though that doesn't stop me from acknowledging that, in terms of uniqueness, there's no other ability that truly compares to Bash.

If you're already tired of 2D side-scrolling games, I recommend you read about these ten best first-person Metroidvanias, ranked.

By 1994 standards, the existence of a video game of Super Metroid's caliber seems absolutely insane, as years have passed and the game remains so fresh and enjoyable that it's astonishing how ahead of its time it was.

People usually praise the Shinespark or the Morph Ball, which are masterful abilities by any measure, but I have to admit that what truly amazed me was the Grapple Beam and how wonderfully it utilizes Zebes' layout.

You spend much of the journey wondering what those strange cross-shaped blocks are for, and when you discover that they allow you to aim a beam that lets you swing freely, spin, and propel yourself at top speed, you can't help but be blown away.

Whether you played it back then or today, Super Metroid surprises you with its mastery at every turn, and there are so many ways I could have included it on this list that all I can do is stand up and applaud.

I'm going to lean towards modernity to define the top spot on this list and praise the work of MIO: Memories in Orbit, a contemporary masterpiece of the genre whose scale is truly breathtaking.

There are many reasons to say this, but unlocking the Striders, tendrils into legs and arms that can grab any surface to walk on walls, was the moment in its campaign when I realized I wasn't just playing a good Metroidvania.

Even though their use is limited by a stamina bar, thinking about the titanic effort it must have taken to craft each room of its vast map based on the eventual access to this omnipotent movement tool makes me dizzy.

Not only does it have fluid and pristine animation with perfect sound design, but it also blends seamlessly with exploration and platforming mechanics to make everything related to movement a true gift for players.

I've had many surprising moments with movement mechanics in the genre, yet the Striders in MIO: Memories in Orbit are unique while also being functional, powerful, and visually appealing, which is an elevated balance that's extremely difficult to achieve.

From the best bosses to essential lore explanations, these are eight Metroidvanias that hide their best content behind the most unexpected paths.

Inside the First Competition Between Historic, Vintage Timepieces

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Want more insider watch coverage? Get Box + Papers, GQ's newsletter devoted to the watch world, sent to your inbox every Friday. Sign up here to get it free.

In the automotive world, high-end cars have gone head-to-head since the early 1920s. Judges didn’t evaluate them based on their speed, though—in a Concours d’Élégance, the prize goes to the best-looking vehicles. The tradition continues today in picturesque sites around the world where collectors submit their often very high-end vintage, classic cars. Surprisingly, there has never been an event for vintage timepieces.

Aurel Bacs, the famous auctioneer whose longstanding association with Phillips has shaped the watch collecting world, endeavored to change that. “It began with a meeting where I said, ‘I have a dream,’” he said in a press release. For the very first iteration of this competition, Bacs and Phillips partnered with Swiss heavyweight Vacheron Constantin. They expected to receive maybe 100 to 200 submissions, but the jury was forced to whittle down 550 of them to 34 finalist watches, whose owners were flown out from across the world for the Concours held at the Hôtel Président in Geneva.

“It was the most beautiful evening in the field ever,” Bacs said. At the Hôtel Président, around 200 attendees gathered, along with a jury of 14 watch-industry experts including Bacs himself, Ben Clymer of Hodinkee, writer and historian Nick Foulkes, longtime watch journalist Roberta Naas, and others. Watches could only be submitted for consideration in one category (Chronographs, Métiers d’Art, Chronomètre Royal, Astronomical Complications, Multiple Complications, Design, and Chiming Mechanisms) and were judged based on nine criteria: authenticity, elegance, rarity, impact on the history of Vacheron Constantin and/or on watchmaking in general, provenance, technicality, métiers d’art (artistic crafts), state of preservation, and emotional dimension.

The jurors deliberated over several hours until the winners of the seven categories and a Best of Show were finally selected. Following a cocktail reception, everyone gathered on the lower floor of the Hôtel Président to see the results from the watch world’s first Concours d'Élégance.

The first category featured a smattering of some of the maison’s most important wristwatches, including a highly rare ref. 4072 in stainless steel from 1940. However, it was a yellow gold pocket-watch chronograph from 1929 that won the day. The result was such a surprise that the audience, intimately familiar with the historical importance of pocket watches, audibly gasped inside the room. It made me wonder if these old-fashioned timepieces could regain their lost position as the pinnacle of watch collecting. This piece certainly warranted the love it received: in excellent condition and featuring a white enamel dial with dual registers in an “up-down” configuration, it’s a beautiful timepiece.

This category celebrates handcrafts such as enameling and gem-setting, and it featured a smattering of truly spectacular pieces—mostly sub-40mm wristwatches in precious metals with wild cloisonné or Grand Feu champlevé enamel dials depicting landmasses or animals.

However, a stunning pocket watch set with blue sapphires throughout the bezel and Roman indices won out.

When an older American gentleman stepped on stage to collect his prize, he explained to a stunned crowd that not only had his great-grandfather, Dr. Otto von Frangeš, been given the watch by His Majesty Alexander I of Yugoslavia, its original owner, but that he wore it while sitting in the motorcade during the king’s assassination in France in 1934. Otto’s middle son, Nikola, eventually smuggled the watch out of Yugoslavia by sewing it into the lining of his wife’s jacket as they fled the Iron Curtain in the late 1940s.

In 1907, Vacheron launched the Chronomètre Royal sub-brand, specifically built to endure a life sailing the seas. The model’s parts were anti-magnetic and resisted rusting. The dials were made out of enamel, which could also stand up to the moist atmosphere without rusting. Because they were critical to maritime navigation, timing precision was of the utmost importance, and models from this line frequently won observatory trials (which tested for a watch’s accuracy) that ran from the 1800s to the mid-1900s. Given all that information, the winner is a bit of a shocker: a ref. 2215 from 1976 that exemplifies the qualities of the line but modernizes them in many ways. It doesn’t even feature a full set of numerals, trading them out for a few diamond-set indices. In his acceptance speech, the owner said he wears it on the weekends with jeans.

The Astronomical Complications category is dear to the heart of anyone who appreciates a good moonphase display and also includes timepieces equipped with a triple-calendar, a complete calendar, or a perpetual calendar. Though more modern pieces, such as a skeletonized QP wristwatch from 1987, made it to the final round, it was a wildly neat (and unique) platinum-cased “Cioccolatone” from 1957 that won out. Housed in a 34mm x 43mm rectangular case and equipped with a complete calendar, it’s owned by a dapper Italian collector, who beamed as he collected the prize with his grown son. This would not be this pair’s first time at the podium…

This is a loaded category for Vacheron, which famously makes the most complicated pocket watch and wristwatch in the world, with 57 and 41 complications, respectively. To be eligible in this category, watches were required to have at least two complications—child’s play for VC.

Spanning everything from an enamel-dialed, minute-repeating, split-seconds chronograph pocket watch from 1901 to several Patrimony watches from the 1990s, the winner was the “Don Pancho.” Delivered in 1940, this diminutive yellow-gold timepiece with minute repeater, day, and retrograde date indications was once the world’s most complicated wristwatch. Double-signed by Brooking in Madrid, it sold for roughly $740,000 at auction back in 2019.

A more subjective category, perhaps, than the others, Design included watches “featuring Vacheron Constantin’s signature design, either in the case or through the time display.” One entrant, for example—a wild time-only ref. 4695 dress watch—featured incredibly intricate lugs and a Maltese Cross-type dial. But the winner was nothing short of revelatory. Once again owned by the aforementioned tall Italian gentleman, the possibly unique ref. 6213 is an automatic world timer from 1957 in yellow gold. Based upon Louis Cottier’s famous system, it is so rare that even Clymer admitted he had never heard of it before the competition. The owner’s son described his father returning home with it one day when he was a boy, excited beyond belief that he had finally tracked it down after years of searching.

This category included pocket watches or wristwatches fitted with a minute repeater, quarter repeater, half-quarter repeater, or grand sonnerie—a mechanism that automatically strikes the hours and quarter-hours every 15 minutes. Incredibly, among the entrants was a red gold quarter-repeater pocket watch from the early 1820s with a stunning guilloché dial. However, the winner was another pocket watch dating to 1926 and cased in platinum. Its ultra-thin design, black Breguet numerals, Breguet blued steel handset, and simple silver dial belied the complex mechanism within. The visibly emotional American gentleman who owns it recalled how, as a boy, he would open a chest of drawers, remove his grandfather’s pocket watch, and listen to its minute repeater.

A bonus category, Best of Show went to a truly spectacular wristwatch: a ref. 6448 in platinum with diamond-set indices. Seemingly a simple time-only dress watch, this tremendous piece—which was produced in just three examples in the early 1960s—boasts a hand-wound, 13-ligne minute-repeater movement. Housed in a brushed-finish case with incredible teardrop lugs, it’s the only known example in this metal, and it was notably the last minute-repeating reference produced by the brand for nearly 30 years.

Nominally, the crowd at the awards ceremony was there to judge the merits of a watch, but I left emotionally moved by the stories each watch owner shared about their timepieces. As the very first concours for watches, the format will no doubt be repeated in the future—either by Vacheron, adopted by other brands, or done as a more traditional multi-brand event like the Concours d’Élégance at Pebble Beach.
