‘The Rings of Power’ Just Dropped a Major Connection to Tolkien’s Deepest Middle-earth Lore

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Editor's note: The below contains spoilers for The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power Season 3 Episode 3.

Anyone who has read J.R.R. Tolkien's The Lord of the Rings or watched the films may recall that Gandalf names himself a "servant of the Secret Fire." It's a mysterious line, one that is never truly unpacked in Tolkien's epic trilogy. However, this season of The Rings of Power expands on the idea by leading Gandalf (Daniel Weyman) to the Flame Imperishable itself in a “burning bush” moment reminiscent of the biblical story of Moses — and the particulars of this scene offer the best understanding of what this spiritual flame truly is.

Although the Secret Fire (voiced by Allan Corduner) never appears as an actual burning bush in Tolkien's story, Gandalf's encounter in The Rings of Power Season 3 isn't far off from the source material, as the cosmic power originally manifests as a seemingly eternal tongue of fire in the desert. "I am that which burns within all things yet still remains unseen," the Flame reveals. "I built the heavens and stars, and all of Middle-earth is cradled within my flame." Although Tolkien was careful not to insert overt religious allegory into his work, he also maintained (as recorded in The Letters of J.R.R. Tolkien) that "The Lord of the Rings is, of course, a fundamentally religious and Catholic work; unconsciously so at first, but consciously in the revision." Many of these undertones exist through certain characters, but on occasion, those ideas are more overt. This is where the Secret Fire comes into play.

As explained in The Silmarillion, the Fire — also called the Flame of Anor in The Fellowship of the Ring or the Flame Imperishable in other sources — is the literal divine power of Eru Ilúvatar, the creator of Tolkien’s fantasy world. It’s not a simple Force-like power that Ilúvatar commands, however, but rather a living extension of himself. As recorded in The Silmarillion, "Ilúvatar gave to their vision Being, and set it amid the Void, and the Secret Fire was sent to burn at the heart of the World; and it was called Eä." In essence, the Secret Fire is the living word and will of Ilúvatar, an aspect of him that exists in all creation itself. The Secret Fire is what gives life to all of Middle-earth, and the desire to attain it is what led Melkor to rebel and become Morgoth in the first place. Since only Ilúvatar can command the Fire, the two are mysteriously but divinely linked.

The cast and creators break down the show's most personal storyline yet for Tolkien's most iconic wizard.

Considering Tolkien's faith, one can draw parallels between the Secret Fire and the Holy Spirit, the third figure of the Trinity, as described in the Bible. In his book, Tolkien and The Silmarillion, Clyde S. Kilby recalled, "Professor Tolkien talked to me at some length about the use of the word 'holy' in The Silmarillion. Very specifically he told me that the 'Secret Fire sent to burn at the heart of the World' in the beginning was the Holy Spirit." Of course, the Holy Spirit is often compared to fire in both the Old and New Testaments, notably during Pentecost, when "tongues of fire" appeared on Jesus Christ's disciples when baptized by the Spirit in Acts 2:3-4, while Hebrews 12:29 describes God metaphorically as a "consuming fire." No doubt, Tolkien had this in mind.

While previous seasons of The Rings of Power have hinted at Eru Ilúvatar's existence, this is the first actual depiction of his presence within the story. Indeed, Tolkien didn't write much about how Ilúvatar directly inserted himself into the rest of creation. While the Secret Fire reveals the truth about why Gandalf was sent to Middle-earth, this is certainly not a commonplace experience. Just as the Valar themselves are still clothed with mystery, so is Ilúvatar — though Tolkien has always asserted that he has been intimately involved in the form of providence. Indeed, according to the author, Gollum's most tragic defeat and the One Ring's destruction during The Lord of the Rings was itself an act of divine intervention by "The Other Power [who] then took over: the Writer of the Story (by which I do not mean myself)," per The Letters of J.R.R. Tolkien.

Nevertheless, most interactions with the Secret Fire (and Ilúvatar himself) throughout Middle-earth come in the form of visions, providence, and power rather than "burning bush" moments. Just as Gandalf considers himself a "servant" of the Flame Imperishable, so too do the Faithful in Númenor. This is part of their tension with the King's Men, as the Faithful still follow the teachings of the Valar, themselves angelic spirits working in Ilúvatar's service, whereas the King's Men rebelled against those traditional ways. It's no wonder that Lloyd Owen, who plays Elendil, once described his character as a "combination of Noah and Job from the Old Testament." As The Rings of Power Season 3 continues to expand to the far reaches of Tolkien's world, the advent of the Secret Fire honors the author who first brought Middle-earth to life.

New episodes of The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power Season 3 premiere Wednesdays on Prime Video.

Germany U21 vs. Belgium U21 preview: Team news and predicted lineups

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Antonio Di Salvo's Germany U21 side entertain Belgium in a friendly game on Friday as both sides already booked their places for next summer's European championship. After their last home game of 2026, Germany will face Croatia in Rijeka four days later in another friendly.

The three-time Euros winners finished ahead of Greece in their qualifying group after eight successive wins. Belgium only needed a point against Denmark in the last matchday to finish top of their group, and they achieved that with a goalless draw in Vejle.

Germany's last success in the U21 Euros came in 2021, but they lost both home and away to Belgium during the qualifying campaign for the tournament hosted by Hungary and Slovenia. Loïs Openda, who later played at RB Leipzig, scored three goals in those two games.

For the captain Nicolo Tresoldi, the final two games of the year could be his last at this level. The striker who scored 16 goals in 27 appearances is yet to make his decision on representing either Germany or his birth country Italy at senior level. The sought-after Club Brugge youngster only trails Heiko Herrlich (17) and Pierre Littbarski (18) in the all-time scoring list for Germany U21.

The former won Bundesliga and Champions League with Borussia Dortmund, while the wing wizard Littbarski played a key role when West Germany won the World Cup in 1990. Tresoldi already showed his potential as he scored 18 goals for club and country since the start of the season, including five in four Champions League games.

Di Salvo has to find solutions for several injury absentees as HSV's Kofi Amoako and Maurice Krattenmacher, who could return to FC Bayern after his excellent start to the season with Elversberg, are set to make their U21 debuts. Both players regularly featured for their clubs so far in the Bundesliga.

In addition to Tresoldi, AS Monaco's Paris Brunner and Dženan Pejčinović will hope to continue their good forms for Germany U21 in front of goal. However, the latter is yet to score his maiden Bundesliga goal for VfB Stuttgart.

Former Kaiserslautern striker Normann Bassette, who is the joint-Belgian Pro League top-scorer alongside Tresoldi, scored five times for Belgium during the Euros qualification campaign.

Hoffenheim's highly-rated attacker Nathan De Cat is unavailable due to injury, but Samuel Mbangula will be back to Germany after his unsuccessful season at Werder Bremen. Former BVB winger Julien Duranville also returns to Belgium U21 squad after making his first few appearances for Lyon.

Belgium: Brughmans; Burlet, Smets ©, Noubi, De Roeve; Mbangula, Van De Peere, Fernandez, Fofana; Vermant, Bassette

Reported Death of Uyghur Woman Detained Since Age 14

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

The girl in the picture looking straight into the camera with probing eyes is 15-year-old Rahile Omer. She was photographed in March 2018 among hundreds of other detainees at a political re-education camp in Konasheher county, Kashgar, Xinjiang. A 2022 BBC investigation identified her as the youngest among the hundreds whose headshots were leaked in the Xinjiang Police Files. Previous records show authorities detained her at age 14 shortly after her parents’ detention in mid-2017.

Click to expand Image Then-15-year-old Rahile Omer photographed at a political re-education camp in Kashgar, in China’s Xinjiang region, March 2018. © Victims of Communism Memorial Foundation

On November 6, Radio Free Asia (RFA) reported that Rahile, at 23, had died in Peyazawat women’s prison while serving an unjust 12-year sentence.

Human Rights Watch has not been able to independently verify this information, but the report should raise alarm about what is happening to the countless Uyghurs who remain behind bars as part of the Chinese government’s crimes against humanity since 2016.

Authorities sentenced her in 2021, when she turned 18, reportedly for “disturbing social order,” according to RFA. Chinese officials familiar with her situation told RFA that she was transferred to prison because she did not provide information about others and was caught praying at the dormitory during her “re-education.” They said she possibly died of a heart problem.

Hundreds of Uyghurs in the diaspora are mourning Rahile. As a member of this community, I fear receiving similar heartbreaking news every day. My father, Memet Yaqup, is serving a 16-year prison sentence; my uncle, Emet Yaqup, is serving life. I do not know their exact locations or the charges against them, nor do I know if they are still alive.

The last time I got word about them was in 2024, when a source told me, “You should be thankful that your father and uncle are still alive, we have lost many lives over the years.”

Global attention has led Beijing to strictly control information from Xinjiang. Authorities deny independent observers, including United Nations human rights experts, unfettered access to the region while organizing carefully choreographed official tours for select friendly diplomats, journalists, social media influencers, and members of the Uyghur diaspora to present a sense of normalcy to the outside world.

Rahile Omer punctuates this narrative, and governments should press Beijing to answer the question: What happened to her?

‘Beyond Anything I Could Have Imagined’: Carl Cox Presented With OBE By Prince William

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

The pioneering DJ and record producer was honored during a ceremony at Windsor Castle.

Oh yes, oh yes! Carl Cox has been presented with his OBE (Officer of the Order of the British Empire), with Prince William on royal duties.

The pioneering British DJ and record producer is recognized for his outstanding services to music, and was one of several music figures identified in the King’s Birthday Honours List back in June of this year.

King Charles’ first born, William, had the honor of presenting Cox with his award during a ceremony this week at Windsor Castle. Related Bella Kay Teases New Music In Billboard 'Up & Coming' Podcast Series Premiere: 'It's Kinda Giving Deluxe' It's Looking Like Oasis Will Skip Their Own Rock Hall of Fame Induction: 'It's Too Far To Be Going For the Night' Brothers Osborne, Luke Bryan, Vince Gill, Halestorm & More Added As 2026 CMA Awards Performers Explore Carl Cox See latest videos, charts and news

“I’m not quite sure I can put into words what today means to me,” Cox writes on his social channels. “Music has been my whole life. From those early days of falling in love with records and DJing, to travelling the world and sharing that love with so many people, I’ve never stopped being grateful that I get to do what I do.”

To receive his OBE from Prince William at Windsor Castle, “recognising nearly 50 years of doing something I absolutely love,” Cox writes, “is beyond anything I could have imagined.”

Cox isn’t just a DJ. He’s an institution, an integral part of the house and techno electronic music story, one of the first “superstar” DJs and a vinyl technician who mastered the three-deck and four-deck mixing techniques. He founded his label Intec Records in 1999, and is a familiar face behind the decks at the biggest festivals and clubs, everywhere.

As clubland remained in lockdown during the pandemic, Cox used the time wisely to complete his autobiography, “Oh Yes, Oh Yes!,” a reference to his catchphrase. The book dropped in August 2021, published by White Rabbit.

“I’ve been thinking a lot about my mum and dad today,” he says of his OBE. “I know just how proud they would have been, and I wish they could have been here to share this moment with me.

“This honour means so much, not just to me personally, but because of where our music has come from and how far it has travelled. To see dance music recognised in this way is something I’m immensely proud of.”

Signing off, he gives a shout out to “family, friends, everyone who has believed in me, supported me and danced with me over the years, thank you. You’ve all been part of this. My heart is very full today. I’m proud, humbled and deeply honoured. Thank you.”

Jack River Signs With G.Y.R.O., Releases First New Music In Three Years

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

"Borderline" is Jack River's first release since 2023's "Endless Summer."

The independent Australian singer, songwriter and advocate (real name: Holly Rankin) returns with a new deal via G.Y.R.O. (Get Your Record Out), and new music in “Borderline.”

A lowkey electronic earworm, “Borderline” is Jack River’s first release since 2023’s Endless Summer, during which she performed over 20 shows while pregnant with her first daughter Maggie. Explore Jack River See latest videos, charts and news

“Borderline” was written following her breakthrough night out on her own, capturing the emotions, the fears, the vulnerability and the strength that comes with rediscovering the world. Related Bella Kay Teases New Music In Billboard 'Up & Coming' Podcast Series Premiere: 'It's Kinda Giving Deluxe' It's Looking Like Oasis Will Skip Their Own Rock Hall of Fame Induction: 'It's Too Far To Be Going For the Night' Brothers Osborne, Luke Bryan, Vince Gill, Halestorm & More Added As 2026 CMA Awards Performers

“This is a song about traveling beyond the borderline, back to one’s self, back to freedom,” she explains. “I wrote this song after one of my first nights alone in three years, overwhelmed with the feeling that my sense of self was returning, and the reckoning that comes with that. It’s the beginning of a new era for Jack River – with less rules, less constraints, and more independence.”

There’s more on the way. Rankin is hard at work on a collection of songs which will traverse those years of “change and transition,” in her “relationships, her career and her personal world,” reads a statement announcing the new project. “Motherhood brought fearlessness, conviction and a realization that there’s no time to mess around, bringing with it new discipline and boundaries, but also a sense of freedom.”

Jack River established her as one of the Australian music scene’s leading lights with her 2018 debut album Sugar Mountain, which snagged three ARIA Award nominations (breakthrough artist of the year, best pop release and engineer of the year), an AIR Award nomination (best independent pop album) and an APRA Award nomination (breakthrough songwriter of the year).

Andy Irvine, CEO and co-founder of G.Y.R.O., the Brisbane-based independent music distribution and label services specialist, welcomes Jack River to its roster. “Holly has a clear vision for her music and an unwavering determination to make the Australian industry better for artists,” he remarks. “The team at G.Y.R.O. are excited to harness that independent spirit and bring her new music to audiences here and around the world.”

Petit Planet - 5 reasons why it's definitely not another Animal Crossing

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Petit Planet offers a gentler, kinder, and more considerate world - and right now, maybe that's exactly what we need

I felt seen. That's generally the vibe I got from Petit Planet during my time with it, but honestly, it might not seem so obvious at first glance. For one thing, the cosy life sim is extremely similar to Nintendo's Animal Crossing, and for another, simulating a virtual life outside the daily grind is nothing new.

But at the HoYoverse Media Tour 2026 in Seoul, I got the chance to get to know Petit Planet with all its complexities and so-called deliberate "imperfections" on a more personal level, and these apparent flaws are, in essence, what makes it more beautiful - much like in real life.

Now, I already did my time as a Planet Tender six months ago during Petit Planet's initial beta, but after going hands-on with the life sim again and experiencing the advanced content during the event, I've come away with a handful of reasons why I now see it's not just another entry into an already saturated genre. It's a tad unfortunate that it took a second go for it to dawn on me, but don't they say that love is sweeter the second time around?

You're responsible for your rose planet The first and likely most obvious difference between this and the aforementioned Nintendo property is the fact that the entire world is, well, a world - albeit on a (seemingly) much smaller scale. You're a Planet Tender, responsible for tending to your very own mini-planet across a charming, spherical space. When I spoke to the Petit Planet devs, they mentioned that this is one of their core design principles, which is why despite the technical challenges that come with rendering something like this (not to mention the possibility of players getting motion sickness), the team pushed themselves to make it work to the best of their ability.

And it really does work. Navigating your mini-planet feels lovingly personal, like you're really tending to something that's truly your own, making every crop you harvest, fish you catch, and decor you place much more intentional. I definitely see what HoYoverse is trying to do there - by shrinking the scale down to a smaller and more manageable size, they're able to evoke all sorts of feels. It's essentially an exercise in learning to be more mindful, which is exactly Petit Planet's theme. Life sim, not management sim The team was quick to reiterate that there's a big difference between a life sim and a management sim, because Petit Planet is definitely not the latter. Your self-contained world aside, dailies don't give you any FOMO; they're less pressure-filled, and you're not meant to speedrun through your tasks like they're chores. Mobai, the official "tutorial" Neighbor who's a totally chill sheepdog, even tells you there's no mortgage to pay back here, poking fun at demanding NPCs other management sims have.

I feel like this ties perfectly well with the more emotional aspect of your smaller home - giving it all kinds of TLC never feels like a burden, because you're never pressured to compete or perform, never hounded to optimise numbers, never pushed or overwhelmed by the to-do list that's practically breathing down your neck. It's a gentler, kinder, and more considerate experience, which brings me to my next point. Consent is everything One major thing I noticed was that the system is almost always asking for your permission, which, as an introvert, I appreciate so darn much. I'm someone who struggles with a bit of phone anxiety, as every time the phone rings - no matter who's calling - I get a mini-panic attack for a split second. I've never really fully understood why that's so, but I suppose it has something to do with how intrusive and sudden a phone call can be to an introvert's alone time.

In essence, Petit Planet - while offering robust experiences for both solo players and social butterflies alike - doesn't really shove the multiplayer aspect down your throat. Social systems are opt-in and not mandatory, so you can choose to go at it alone if you ever feel the need to recharge your social battery all by yourself. You can allow or deny other players from visiting or taking resources from your planet, and you can even choose the stage of your relationships whenever you see someone else's planet floating by overhead.

I found this incredibly refreshing, especially with the Epistolarian's Desk. It's a letter-writing system where you can write notes and send them out there into the Starsea, and whether or not you receive a reply is never certain. I love how melancholy this whole act is - the idea of sending a letter to someone and never quite knowing if you'll ever get a response back. It's bittersweet and beautiful, and one of the ways I feel Petit Planet invites you to slow down and be more mindful in life.

So yes, the loneliness is real, but there's comfort in knowing someone's out there going through the same thing too. Live service without the grind But probably the most striking thing that makes Petit Planet stand out is the fact that it's a live service game, which is an interesting mix. Yes, it's all about slowing down, but it's also got constant updates to keep things fresh - and I guess I'll just have to wait until the official launch to see how that goes. Updates are supposedly going to be released at a more relaxed pace, with a focus on daily pleasures and fresh surprises you'll get to experience every time you log in.

For example, I was able to play through the winter content for the advanced build I played at the event, and there was a whole bunch of stuff to do for the seasonal update. But again, I didn't feel pressured to cross everything off my checklist of side-quests and missions - I found that I could simply wander around and kick snowballs at random things if I wanted to, just because. The new Neighbors are a lovely touch, of course, and if you ever feel like you want to get to know them a little bit better, you definitely can - or not; it's all up to you. Neighbors aren't pets Given that this is a HoYoverse game, I know some people might think that the Neighbors are part of a gacha system where you have to pull to collect them all, but the NPCs here aren't pets you need to unlock to progress. Neighbors have their own different personalities, quirks, and homes, with interactions you can trigger (even among each other) depending on certain events. They remember answers too and have their own preferences for their home decor, so they're definitely not a thing you own!

And if they're not part of the gacha, this means that monetisation is purely for cosmetics only, which is in line with the low-key nature of the sim. Even the Starsea Voyage - a mode where you can go around and explore uncharted lands for lovely little discoveries - no longer uses up resources, as you can simply wander the Starsea and do some introverting if you want to. It's all about relaxing and not managing resources, after all, which is also evident in how you navigate the terrain around you.

Interestingly, the devs included deliberate "imperfections" in the way you control your character. Controls are a tad clumsy in some places, as they wanted to avoid entirely automated affairs to force you to pay more attention. I did notice this while I was playing, as I found I couldn't just randomly scale higher ledges or climb through them just to get to where I needed to go - I had to really go around and look for the stairs or the right pathways, making things more intentional.

I'm curious to see how this will resonate with players, as clumsy controls run the risk of frustrating everyone. But if it pays off, then it's a massively effective way of keeping players chill on a more subconscious level - and HoYoverse has always been big on taking risks.

Suffice it to say I'm looking forward to the official launch on December 4th, which says a lot since I've never really been a fan of the genre. Whether or not Petit Planet changes all that remains to be seen, but I'm definitely willing to voyage into the Starsea to find out.

Interview: Petit Planet dev team talks life vs management, and the ultimate introvert's dream

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Time to stop and smell the roses, so to speak

So-called "cosy" life sims are a dime a dozen these days, but HoYoverse is looking to throw its hat in the ring with a different kind of approach to your average entry in the genre. For one thing, it's supposedly a true life sim, not a management sim. And for another, it's really all about slowing down and unwinding at the end of the day, not looking for an alternate life apart from your own.

Honestly, that kind of low-pressure vibe feels like exactly what I need at the moment to help me deal with the hustle and bustle of everyday life, and over the past week, we've had the pleasure of spending a few days with the Petit Planet team to share with us what that's all about.

It was a privilege to hear lead game designer Murphy and lead artist Yinli Huang's thoughts at the roundtable discussion we had during the media tour in Seoul, South Korea, including their own hopes and dreams for the life sim (I've edited their answers for clarity and brevity, by the way). It's interesting to see such a different take on the genre, for instance, especially since the entire "world" is structured into mini-planets rather than your typical town or city. It was initially made as a flat space, but it wasn't in line with the team's core design.

"As a life simulation game, we decided to use some rendering techniques to present a feeling of that sphere. That indeed brought a lot of challenges technically and visually." One of those challenges was actually how to make it so that players don't feel any kind of motion sickness across this spherical world.

And it's certainly a very different style when compared to other HoYoverse games. "It's very important for HoYoverse to explore different genres, and with a life simulation game, it's the kind of genre where we can build an emotional connection with players and the community. And from HoYoverse's past experiences, we've accumulated a lot of knowledge in terms of technology and the ability to build out narratives and characters. We believe that those capabilities developed from our past games can bring brand-new experiences for players of this genre."

This is evident in the difference from, say, Animal Crossing, as the team believes Petit Planet is, as I mentioned, a life sim and not a management sim. It's meant to be cosier and less pressure-filled, which, as someone who doesn't have the patience to grind through a management sim, I greatly appreciate. But what makes Petit Planet still inherently HoYoverse is, as the team puts it, that quality that players have come to know and expect from the brand.

"We always try to provide high-quality games to our players, and that is also what Petit Planet wants to do. And in Petit Planet, we've also tried to explore something new - the multiplayer gaming experience and also a social system - and that is something unique to this game."

Still, from what I've played, it did feel like the single-player aspect is robust enough as it is, which is interesting for a life sim like this. "For us, it's very important to provide a good gaming experience for both types of players. For those who prefer to play solo, there are always settings that will allow them to turn off those multiplayer experiences, but once they feel comfortable or they want to explore the social part of the game, they can always go to the Galactic Bazaar and also use the Epistolarian's Desk. But the core gameplay will be largely the same for both types of players," they explained.

"We have adopted certain technical measures to make sure the multiplayer experience will go as smoothly as possible. For example, when a player gets to the Galactic Bazaar, the multiplayer settings will adjust dynamically based on the number of players. But there will also be some limits when players visit other planets - you can choose to allow or ban players from taking resources from your planet."

Which actually brings me to the most interesting bit for me with my hands-on experience, which is the aforementioned Epistolarian's Desk. I asked them what the inspiration or story behind that was, because I felt like that was what resonated with me the most as an introvert. I really like the idea of writing a letter and the uncertainty of whether or not you'll receive a reply, and seeing all the letters that all of the people have written so far.

There's just something so heartbreaking and tragic - yet still so inherently beautiful about it.

I told the team that the system felt very considerate, as you're always asking for permission if people are allowed to reply or if you're going to accept their reply. I just wanted to know the inspiration behind the idea, because I don't think I've seen that anywhere else before.

"It's a kind of asynchronous design on purpose, because that kind of system won't bring our players, especially introverts, too much pressure. We've also looked at similar references in other places, but we found that players will message randomly, so we tried to set up a formal setting in the game to make the action more like a ritual. This means that people can then write a letter and take it more seriously," the team shared. "We designed the exchange on purpose because we found that the constant need to interact might put pressure on players. Once you become friends online in a game, you will need to invest a lot of energy and time to sustain the friendship, so we want to avoid that in Petit Planet."

This actually extends further to dailies and seasonal content - with Petit Planet being a live service sim, how can the team make sure the dailies aren't a grind and still keep seasonal content fresh? Mobai, the NPC sheepdog, even jokes that there's no mortgage here, after all.

"We'd like to use the fishing system as an example. So, in some other games, you might develop skills and levels for your fishing skills. But for Petit Planet, whether you're a new player or a veteran player, the fish that you get is the same - it's random. So there's no growth curve; we want to make it simulate real life, so the fish you get will vary depending on the season or the weather. Our purpose is to provide a little surprise for players every time they log in to the game, so that they will have the incentive to log in and enjoy the experience."

The narrative goes hand in hand here as well. "Unlike other HoYoverse games, there's no such thing as a 'main plot'. The whole narrative will be more fragmented - for example, if you look at the descriptions for the items, there will be a lot of content or stories behind them. We've also developed a very comprehensive backstory of each of the Neighbors - that is also part of the main narrative, because we want to make this a more livable world."

Now, all that cosiness is all well and good, but one of the things I was also curious about, especially with my experience of the beta, was how the controls worked in this supposedly more intentional, mindful world. I know that developers said they designed some of the controls to be "less automatic" - for lack of a better term - to force players to pay closer attention to what they're doing, which is something I also observed while playing.

For instance, I noticed that you really can't simply jump across ledges, as you really need to go around to find the door or the right pathway should you need to. So I asked them how they're able to balance those "less automatic" controls with making sure things aren't frustrating for players when the controls start to get wonkier than usual.

"In terms of controls and cameras, we did a lot of research and considered different solutions, from the most limited ones to allowing players to freely explore the landscape and environment. We thought of different degrees of freedom for players while also keeping the imperfections for the life simulation. The team debated different solutions for, say, climbing. Initially, internally, we discussed how many layers a player can climb, but there will be different creatures on the lower level and higher level, so if players can jump multiple layers at once, that will remove the joy of exploring and the sense of surprise. So there are deliberate considerations behind the control of players in Petit Planet."

With that, we wrapped things up - and I do have to say that so far, Petit Planet is shaping up to be a dream come true. I've never really connected with life sims and management sims before, as I've always found the fetch quests and social aspects a tad too grindy for me. Thankfully, Petit Planet seems to be offering a gentler way for us all to enjoy a virtual world without the demands that usually come with it, and with its launch on December 4th, we'll get to see if that is indeed the case.

For now, if you're curious about how it plays, I did a quick preview of Petit Planet when it was in its beta phase earlier this year, and I'll have a proper review ready in time for its release soon too. And since its monetisation is supposedly purely for cosmetics only, I'd say it's worth giving a go - especially if you're the ultimate introvert like me.

Spot The Papaya: Etihad’s McLaren F1-Liveried Boeing 787 Hits The Skies

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Etihad Airways has revealed a stunning new livery for one of its Boeing 787-10 Dreamliner widebody twinjets. The Abu Dhabi-based UAE flag carrier has chosen to paint the aircraft in a distinctive orange and black color scheme, with the bulk of the jet sporting the famous 'papaya' shade worn by the McLaren Racing Formula One Team. The livery was teased this year as Etihad and McLaren teamed up.

Etihad unveiled the new livery on the Boeing 787-10 Dreamliner, which bears the registration A6-BMI, at a glamorous event at its engineering facilities in Abu Dhabi. As well as celebrating the first time that a widebody jet has featured an F1 team's livery, the event also played host to a successful Guinness World Record attempt for the largest single-piece kabuki reveal. The jet re-enters service today.

The McLaren name is one of the oldest and most iconic in F1, and, over the years, its cars have worn a wide range of legendary liveries. Most recently, however, the team's name has been associated with the striking 'papaya' shade of orange, which it has worn since 2017, including in McLaren driver Lando Norris's 2025 championship-winning season. Now, Etihad's Boeing 787-10 shares this color.

Etihad describes the orange and black paint job as "one of the most prominent motorsport liveries ever carried by a commercial aircraft and a new way for guests and fans to connect with McLaren Racing." With its 787s serving a wide range of destinations all around the world, there will certainly be no shortage of attention for the striking orange scheme. Antonoaldo Neves, Etihad Airways' CEO, said that:

"Etihad and McLaren Racing are two ambitious global brands with a shared focus on performance and a drive to keep moving forward. This aircraft will proudly carry that story from our home in Abu Dhabi to cities around the world."

While the recent reveal represented the first time that Etihad's new Dreamliner livery saw the light of day, it was actually teased by the Abu Dhabi-based UAE flag carrier earlier this year, more than eight months ago. At the end of February 2026, the airline announced that it had become an official partner of McLaren Racing, supporting both its Formula One and United Autosports WEC Hypercar teams.

With this in mind, in its role as an Official Partner of McLaren Racing, Etihad's logo is emblazoned all over the team's cars and helmets, such as on the MCL40’s rear wing and halo. The carrier has long had close ties to Formula One, having been the title sponsor of the Abu Dhabi Grand Prix since 2009. However, putting pen to paper on a deal to become an Official Partner of McLaren shifted its role up a gear.

On this front, McLaren Racing CEO Zak Brown welcomed the livery reveal as the latest development in his team's work with Etihad Airways, saying that "it's fantastic to see our partnership with Etihad Airways brought to life in such a unique way." He added that "the livery brings together two iconic global brands and will connect McLaren Racing and Etihad with fans and customers around the world."

The Abu Dhabi-based carrier will also be launching a special livery Boeing 787.

According to Flightradar24, A6-BMI spent around a month on the ground as it received its new paint job. As seen in the animated route map above, flight tracking data shows that its last pre-repaint flight took place on October 11, as the Boeing 787-10 flew from Jakarta Soekarno Hatta International Airport (CGK) in Indonesia back to Abu Dhabi International Airport (AUH) in the UAE as flight EY475.

Now that the jet has received its stunning new livery and been welcomed back into the fleet following the aforementioned extravagant ceremony, Madrid Barajas Airport (MAD) will be its first destination, with a round trip (EY103/104) operating on Tuesday, November 10. This will be followed by rotations to Phuket (HKT) in Thailand on November 11 and Dublin Airport (DUB) in Ireland on November 12.

How to check how many chests you’ve opened in Fortnite Treasure Hunters

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Looting is more important than ever before in Fortnite’s Treasure Hunters mini-season, as there are lots of freebies up for grabs this time around, including access to everything in the shop if you get really lucky. Because of this, you may be searching for a way to keep track of how many chests you’ve opened.

Finding and opening chests is the only way to get your hands on quite a few rare items this season, so keeping an eye on all of the work you’ve put in is a helpful tool for tracking progress. It can be tricky to find on your own, though, so here’s how to check how many chests you’ve opened in Fortnite.

You can keep track of how many chests you’ve opened in Fortnite Treasure Hunters from the Quests tab. This helpful mechanic can be easy to miss, as there are quite a lot of missions to tackle listed in this section. Here’s how to find it.

Each of the chest quests listed here tracks your progress across five stages. As you hit each of the five milestones for these bonus goals, you earn extra XP, and the listed maximum number of chests being tracked increases.

These missions only track chests up to 1,000 for each type, so once you hit this number in each of the three categories, your total opened chests will no longer be displayed. This means you can only check your opened chests for the season up until you hit this cap.

Read what’s behind the latest news in gaming (with a bit of snark) by subscribing to the Destructoid Checkpoint newsletter!

The number of chests you open does not directly affect your odds of finding the Pirate Shop Access or other special loot drop items. The loot odds and drop rates are completely unrelated to how many chests you open, as this system is completely based on RNG, which means it comes down to random luck and chance.

Although opening more chests doesn’t directly increase your odds of finding Pirate Shop Access and other loot items, the more you loot, the more shots you have at potentially finding something. This system can be a bit confusing to fully understand, but a good way to conceptualize it is to compare the loot system to a bag of marbles.

Think of all the special loot items as rare, colored marbles in a big bag of grey ones. Each time you open a chest, you’re pulling a marble out. If the marble is grey, it goes right back in. If it’s colored, it stays out.

Your odds of pulling a colored one, which is a special loot item, don’t increase over time, as the grey marbles, or standard loot items, always go back into the pool. At the same time, the more you reach into the bag by opening chests, the more shots you have at finding items.

Although you can’t boost your odds of finding rare drops by opening more chests, you can help the process along by attending special events that feature boosted odds for these rare items. Treasure Hours are the best time to go looting for these assets.

GTA 6's Missing Data Suggests It Might Be Censored In Certain Regions

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Pre-loading for GTA 6 has begun. That means you can put the game on your console right now; you’re just not allowed to play it yet. It also means people who know how to do so can take a peek inside some of the GTA 6 files, and they’ve quickly discovered something interesting.

Actually, they’ve discovered something interesting is missing. Tex2 has listed GTA 6’s PlayGo chunk metadata (thanks, VGC). PlayGo is effectively the PlayStation equivalent of Xbox Smart Delivery, splitting the data into chunks so that those chunks can then be divvied up depending on what a particular system needs.

It’s largely used to divide up what’s needed for last-gen and current-gen versions of the same game. However, it can also be used to remove certain elements of a game in select regions. That appears to be what has happened here, and Tex2 points out that one of those chunks is missing from GTA 6’s metadata in Australia and New Zealand.

GTA 6’s metadata is split into three categories: story, minigame, and core. It’s a core section that has been omitted from the version of GTA 6 being sold Down Under, which suggests it's something out in the game’s massive open world that will be missing.

The size of the missing chunk is small, just 704 KB. Hard to believe there’s anything that small in a game that’s now confirmed to need more than 200 GB of free space, no matter where you plan to play it. That tiny file size suggests whatever it is that’s missing from GTA 6 in Australia and New Zealand, it’s not something anyone is likely to miss.

It’s so small, and GTA 6 is so big, that it might be something we take months to even find.

The file size suggests that this is probably a texture of some sort that has been removed, potentially for legal reasons. Australia somewhat infamously has stricter rules than other parts of the world when it comes to drug use in video games that give you a gameplay advantage. It can, and has, resulted in games being refused classification. Perhaps that missing metadata chunk is linked to that specific kind of drug use in GTA 6.

It’s now only nine days until we can play GTA 6 and try to solve the mystery ourselves. Those playing in Australia and New Zealand will get a head start too, since the game will launch there first due to the time difference. They will need to wait for the rest of us to solve this missing data mystery, though, as they’ll need people outside of that region to know what it is that’s missing.

The theory that codes in boxes are being used to avoid leaks at all costs just became a little bit more credible.

In The Ogre’s Mouth — John Muir Trail Day 12

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

I woke up in my little tent spot nice and early, around 5:30 a.m., and then it took me two hours to get on the trail.  During that time the sun appeared to light the mountain on the opposite side of the valley.

Early morning view from my camp site. I needed Geologist Sam to explain the stripes in the rock…

This time the biggest delay was that the bottom of my tent was quite damp, so I spread it out to dry before packing it and heading on my way.

While I was doing all that, along came Sally who greeted me warmly and then walked on by.  Here’s a little more information about Sally that I learned the previous day:  she was born in Canton, and she works in medical research.  The rest I already reported.

By 7:30 a.m. I started off and immediately started down the infamous Golden Staircase.  I could see how it got its name.  Oh my gosh, it was just a straight down drop of short switchbacks back and forth, and back and forth, and back and forth, seemingly endlessly.  It was enough to make your ears pop from the altitude change.  Going down was bad enough, but I really felt sorry for the poor people trying to ascend it!  I could imagine how it might be demoralizing and exhausting as well.

It’s a little hard to see, but there is a trail going down that near-vertical descent!

Finally, I got to the bottom and eventually I even caught up with Sally.  We stopped for a water break together and then I went past her onto what was the nicest, cruisiest stretch since I started the John Muir Trail.

After the river’s steep drop coming down, the landscape leveled out and the trail mostly followed the gentle contour of the river heading due west.  As a smooth quality trail, it meant I could make very good time.  In the lower elevation the lizards reappeared, and I finally got some pictures of them.

As a child in Mexico City I bred lizards, so I have a special place in my heart for them!

I also  took a picture of a deer, some very large pine cones, as well as shots of several waterfalls.

It feels like somehow they know I am not a predator…

Lower elevations also brought much bigger pine cones.

Mostly, though, I kept a fast pace for about seven miles down the Palisade Creek valley to the river juncture with the Middle Fork Kings River, where the trail turned sharply north and started climbing again.

I reached that juncture at about 10:30 where I rested and had a snack.  Along with the food I took my last dose of antibiotics from the hospital.  I had to admit the medication had been highly effective, but not perfectly so.  I slept through the night now but still coughed occasionally.  I was easily 95% better though and grateful for it!

This was also the spot for errands including filtering water, filling my storage containers, and washing my socks and bandanas.  It was a little challenging to do this as the river was flowing swiftly, but I got the errands done.

My pack resting against a log. (I was resting too…)

As I took stock afterwards, however, I came up one sock liner short and assumed it had washed down the river.  Rather than accept its loss, I went back and looked for it.  To my pleasant surprise, I found I had unwittingly dropped it on the trail before washing.  So, I returned to wash it, then brought it back to its lonely mate.

Looking at those sock liners, I was disappointed.  In the past I considered them indestructible, but now I saw holes forming around the toes and elsewhere, apparently from chafing.  They were still worth using, but I realized they would not endure beyond the end of the hike at best.

Sally arrived at the juncture shortly before I departed.  Today it seemed we were tag-teaming more than we were leapfrogging, but I enjoyed a snack with her and directed her to the water collection site before hoisting my pack.

As I started going upriver, I realized I was at 8,000 feet, my lowest point so far on the JMT northbound route.  The next pass, John Muir Pass, would be almost 4,000 feet higher.  I resolved to try to get more than half of that elevation gain on this day, before making camp.

I was pretty sure I could identify the 10,000-foot contour.  In the Sierras, open fires are not allowed above 10,000 feet.  For this reason, it seemed every time we crossed that elevation there would be a sign flagging the change in fire status.  Most hikers would thereby know whether they were above or below 10,000 feet.

This shot was actually taken on a different day, but it captures what I was talking about, so I inserted it anyway.

As I ascended slowly, I eventually reached the turnoff to Bishop Pass, an indicator that I had gone ten miles already.  I was feeling pretty strong.   Without resting, I drank some water to prevent dehydration and pressed on.  It was a boost of confidence to reach the point I intended to stop, and to realize I still had energy for several miles more.

At a bridge over the stream I looked down and saw three little fishes swimming in an eddy of the stream.  I began to sing “Three little fishies in an itty bitty pool…”  You know that one?  First recorded in 1939, so maybe not.  At that moment a hiking couple arrived and no doubt thought I was completely insane singing to the fishes!

Two of the three little fishies… the third was camera-shy.  I am fairly confident they are Golden Trout!

I continued climbing and climbing through woods that changed as I went higher up.  For a while a number of thorny bushes lined the trail.  In my shorts this was uncomfortable; though they didn’t actually break the skin, they left many surface scratches.

I stopped to fill up my water bottle again at an ideal camp site.  While tempted to stay there, I decided it was still too early to pitch my tent.  Just as I was leaving, a guy came and asked if I’d take his picture on his phone.  I looked across the river and saw a stunning mountain.  “How cool,”  I thought, “I’ll crouch really low, aiming upward, and it will make a great picture with this giant crag looming behind him!”

That was not his intention at all.  It turned out I had not realized it, but I stopped at an iconic trail location.   The man moved toward a giant boulder, and then I saw it.  It was a face!  A face naturally created  by the geology of the place, but with a huge gaping ogre’s mouth.  In that yawning maw, people had placed a row of small triangular stones to represent teeth.  They added a couple on top of his head as silly little ears.

The man entered the “mouth” and lay down between the “teeth.”  He said his girlfriend back in France had taken her picture there several years before, so now I took his picture to send back to her.   Of course, having seen “The Thing” I had to ask him to reciprocate with a picture of me!  How funny that I almost left the site without even noticing it…

The forest was reduced to occasional scraggly trees as I hiked higher, passing more waterfalls as the slope became steeper.

Waterfall catching the sunlight, with the wildflowers in front? Lovely!

Shortly after passing the symbolic 10,000-foot sign (“No Fires!”) I came to a lush meadow.  It looked like a perfect camping area, and I found what seemed like an ideal spot off the trail.

I dropped my pack and turned off my AllTrails app and prepared to settle in.  Then I realized the meadow was flat and green and there were no logs or rocks for me to sit on.  I thought, “Let me wander around here a little nearby.  I’m sure I can find a nice big stone to carry over by my tent to sit on.”  Indeed, I found just such a stone.  But when I picked it up, I found under the stone was… toilet paper!  Ugh!

I put it back down in the same spot quickly and reassessed my decision.  Suddenly that place felt like bad karma, so I turned AllTrails back on again, shouldered my pack, and continued looking for a place to spend the night.

It was late afternoon already, and I worried that the camp sites higher up might all be full.  I decided to poll the descending southbound hikers passing me to find out what I might expect.  My question was simple and uniform:  “How many people did you encounter higher up who had already made camp there?”

The first person who came by answered, “One” and I was relieved.  But the second person who came along answered, “Six.”  I grew concerned.  Finally, the third downhill hikers were two women; they looked like mother and daughter.  To my question, the mother answered, “Fifteen, I think.”   Fifteen!  Then, to my relief, the daughter chimed in, “Oh no, I don’t think so, maybe five?”

This was a quasi-scientific experiment proving how totally unreliable we humans are at reporting anything.  Yet it left me with the same dilemma.  Then the daughter continued, “there’s a pretty site just up ahead and there still seemed to be room there.”

I looked on the AllTrails app, and it did show a spot nearby with four tent sites.   I figured, “Well, even if there are a couple of people there, I don’t mind sharing  and it will give me a little extra elevation and a little extra distance that I won’t have to take on tomorrow.”

Upward I trudged.  Soon, there was the spot with three tents already pitched, but space for a fourth about twenty yards apart from them.  The owners of the tents seemed to be friends, or maybe a family, as they had camp chairs and were all playing cards together.

Late afternoon arrival, with the sky still a deep blue!

I took my spot and pitched my tent, feeling very tired.  As I took off my trail runners and socks, I realized my feet were well beyond filthy.  I wanted a bed and a shower; not necessarily right away, but soon.

That was when I broke down and texted my wife on my Garmin.  It was something like:  “If you can get me a cabin or a room at the John Muir Ranch for day-after-tomorrow, I will be very grateful.  It is probably all booked up, but just in case, please give it a try.”

It was deep twilight by the time I soaked my dinner and dug my cathole.  I wolfed down my repast and called it a night.  It felt like it might prove to be a cold one.

Hi, I’m Papa George! In my 69 years I’ve done a lot of cool things – I’ve crossed the Atlantic in a 40-foot sailboat, bushwacked 100 miles across the north slope of the Alaska Range, hiked for 26 days in Western Nepal crossing five passes over 5,000 meters, and climbed Mt. Kilimanjaro, to name a few. It was my honor to serve my country as a diplomat around the world for 34 years. I have been active in retirement, and I hiked the Wonderland Trail in 2024 and the Long Trail in 2025. This year I'm trying... the John Muir Trail!

Rob and Michele Reiner's Kids Make Rare Appearance Honoring Late Parents

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Rob Reiner's kids are keeping their parents' legacy alive ... stepping into the spotlight for a special night honoring the late Hollywood director and his wife, Michele.

Jake and Romy Reiner made a rare public appearance Sunday night at a gala at the Beverly Wilshire Hotel in Beverly Hills ... where they paid tribute to their parents nearly a year after their shocking deaths.

The siblings took the stage to present family friend Jamie Lee Curtis with the inaugural Michele and Rob Reiner Champion for Change Award ... recognizing her commitment to helping children and families.

Jake told the crowd his parents were always pushing to make the world a better place ... adding their dedication to helping kids started right at home, where they created a loving environment for their own family.

And it wasn't just the Reiner family showing up ... Chelsea Handler hosted the star-studded affair, while John Mayer took the stage for a special performance.

The evening was a huge success, raising a whopping $4 million.

As we first reported, Rob and Michele were found dead inside their Los Angeles home in December 2025.

Their son, Nick Reiner, was charged with two counts of first degree murder in connection with their deaths. He has pleaded not guilty and remains behind bars without bail.

Rocket Rally brings 30-second arcade rally races to iOS and Android

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

You've got half a minute and one thumb to win

When we reviewed Major Mayhem, the iPhone 4S was brand new. Rocket Jump, the studio behind it, is back, this time with Rocket Rally, which is a physics-driven arcade rally racer that’s up for pre-registration on iOS and Android.

If it predates your phone, Major Mayhem was a one-finger cover shooter. Your soldier ran between cover by himself, and you held a finger down to fire. It was brash and silly and very easy to pick up for a minute, and it also earned a Silver Award from us.

Rocket Rally takes that one-input approach to rally. Races last thirty seconds, you play it one-handed in portrait, and the cars run on a custom arcade physics system the studio built itself. Time Attack and Head to Head sit alongside Rally X4 and Rally X6.

Around that is a solo career set in locations around the world, where you buy cars, upgrade them and build up a garage, plus daily online competition. There are over 100 stages across four environments at launch, with more promised every month, and hundreds of cosmetic options for your liveries.

Thirty seconds doesn’t feel like a lot, but I think it’s the right length for something like this. I don’t see myself completing a three-lap race on my phone while I’m en route somewhere, but a rally stage before my bus reaches its stop? I can see myself getting behind that. Of course, that means it leaves no time to learn handling that doesn’t make sense straight away.

Rocket Rally is expected to release on December 15th, although that could change. It will be free-to-play with ads. Pre-registration is currently open on the App Store and Google Play.

If you're into proper racing, most races on our list of the best racing games for iOS take a good deal longer than thirty seconds.

American Airlines Has A Unique Airbus A321neo Subfleet That Unavoidably Breaks Daily Consistency

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Robin Guess | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

American Airlines operates two versions of the Airbus A321neo that look broadly similar to passengers but cannot be made identical. 10 aircraft acquired from Alaska Airlines have a different emergency-exit arrangement from the airline’s factory-delivered A321neos, a structural feature inherited from their original design. Even after American refurbished the former Alaska jets with its standard Oasis cabin, the fuselage layout prevented the airline from matching the larger subfleet’s seating capacity and seat locations.

The result is a small but persistent source of inconsistency across American’s narrowbody operation. Both versions are presented under the same 32Q fleet code, yet they have different total seat counts, exit rows, and cabin details. Passengers selecting seats based on a particular map may therefore find that an aircraft substitution changes the location of extra-legroom seating or places them in a different part of the cabin. For the airline, the challenge is not simply maintaining two interiors. It is operating a single fleet designation across aircraft whose underlying layouts cannot be standardized through a conventional cabin retrofit.

The subfleet traces its origins to Virgin America, which ordered the aircraft before Alaska Airlines acquired the carrier in 2016. Alaska inherited the Airbus jets as part of the transaction, despite operating an otherwise Boeing-focused mainline fleet. When Alaska later moved to simplify its operations around Boeing narrowbodies, it retired the 10 A321neos and sold them to American in 2023. Only four of the 10 aircraft entered service with Virgin America; the other six joined Alaska directly after the acquisition. By the time American took ownership, the aircraft were relatively young, averaging about 5.6 years old when Alaska retired them. Purchasing an existing group offered American access to additional A321neo capacity without waiting for new production slots, which were already constrained by strong global demand for the type.

The aircraft began entering American’s operation in spring 2024, initially flying from Miami with much of Alaska’s original cabin still intact. At that stage, they had 190 seats, including 16 in first class and 174 in the main cabin. The layout offered a more spacious experience than American’s standard A321neo, with greater seat pitch in several sections, but fewer first class places for complimentary upgrades.

American’s acquisition was therefore a practical fleet decision, not a plan to create a permanently distinct passenger product. The airline intended to bring the aircraft closer to its established domestic configuration. However, the jets’ original door arrangement would prove to be the limiting factor. The timing also offered an opportunity to add capacity relatively quickly. New aircraft deliveries require production slots, financing, and time to enter service, whereas the former Alaska jets were already built and had accumulated comparatively little operating history. Their acquisition helped American expand its narrowbody resources while it awaited additional Airbus deliveries. The trade-off was accepting a small group of aircraft whose design would complicate efforts to maintain a uniform cabin.

The core of the problem is the development of Airbus Cabin Flex, or ACF. Early A321neo aircraft were built with a door arrangement broadly resembling the previous-generation A321ceo: four main passenger doors on each side of the fuselage, including an additional pair positioned ahead of the wing. The 10 former Alaska aircraft belong to this earlier A321neo configuration. Airbus introduced Cabin Flex to give airlines more freedom over cabin layouts and passenger capacity. The redesign removed the second pair of full-size doors and introduced modified overwing emergency exits, alongside changes to the rear fuselage and door positions. This created additional usable cabin space and allowed airlines to install more seats while meeting evacuation requirements. Airbus said the ACF arrangement could support configurations of up to 240 passengers, depending on the chosen layout.

The revised design became standard on later A321neo production, but the former Alaska aircraft were built before that transition. Their physical structure cannot be converted into the later Cabin Flex arrangement through a normal interior refurbishment. Changing the exit layout would require extensive structural work, certification, and significant investment, making a full conversion commercially impractical for a group of only 10 aircraft. American’s larger fleet is made up of A321-200NX aircraft, while the former Alaska jets are classified as A321-200N models. The suffix reflects a difference in the airframe configuration, not merely the airline’s choice of seats or cabin finishes.

American Airlines has received four new Airbus jets in one week as its A321neo and A321XLR fleets expand.

American began bringing the former Alaska aircraft into its standard interior in 2024, with N950XV among the first to undergo the work in San Antonio. The retrofit replaced the inherited cabin with the airline’s Oasis-style arrangement, aligning the passenger experience more closely with other domestic A321s. However, the door configuration remained unchanged, limiting how many seats could be installed and where certain rows could be positioned.

After the refurbishment, each former Alaska A321neo has 190 seats. The airline’s 196-seat A321neo configuration has the same number of first class seats, but accommodates more in the main cabin. Six additional economy seats distinguish the larger group. The former Alaska aircraft effectively retain the capacity of American’s older A321ceo domestic layout, despite being newer-generation aircraft with more efficient engines. The original Alaska cabin had fewer first class seats and more generous spacing in several sections, but American’s standardization work increased first class from 16 to 20 seats and brought economy seating closer to the airline’s usual density. Passengers no longer receive the same broad spacing advantage associated with the aircraft’s initial configuration, but the aircraft still cannot carry as many passengers as the ACF-equipped A321neos.

American Airlines Airbus A321neo Cabin Comparison

For American, the six-seat shortfall is a permanent capacity constraint on each of the 10 aircraft. The figure is modest relative to the airline’s overall operation, but it illustrates how a seemingly small design difference can affect revenue potential over thousands of flights. There is also a cabin-planning consequence. American has to accommodate the same general product standards within a narrower seating footprint, rather than simply copying the exact arrangement used on its newer aircraft. The result is a familiar-looking cabin with a different internal map. Passengers may notice little difference in the overall appearance, but the location of individual seats remains tied to the original aircraft design.

The practical consequence is most visible when passengers select seats. American groups both A321neo versions under the same 32Q fleet code, so the booking process does not always make the underlying configuration obvious. Yet the exit rows occupy different positions because the doors and emergency exits are arranged differently. According to SeatCompare’s 2026 guide, the 196-seat A321neo has exit rows at 17 and 27. On the 10 former Alaska aircraft, the corresponding rows are 11 and 24.

The differences extend beyond emergency exits. The seat maps have different last rows, with row 37 on the 196-seat aircraft and row 36 on the former Alaska jets. Lavatory placement also changes the available seats near the rear of the cabin. SeatCompare notes that the port-side lavatory block removes different A-C positions in the two configurations, meaning the cabin columns do not line up in the same way from one aircraft to the other. Some seats are also narrower or have restricted recline, but the affected rows vary by version. As a result, a passenger comparing seat maps needs more than the aircraft name to identify the best option. The registration numbers N950XV through N959XV identify the former Alaska group.

A uniquely liveried Coastliner featuring 20 lie-flat Polaris suites and massive seatback screens readies for transcontinental service.

Maintaining two cabin layouts within one fleet code creates work for American’s reservation systems, airport teams, and customers. Seat maps must accurately reflect the aircraft assigned to a flight, while operational substitutions can make a previously selected seat unavailable or alter its characteristics. While aircraft swaps are a normal part of airline operations, the fleet differences can cause some challenges.

There are also commercial reasons for American to retain the aircraft rather than retire or substantially rebuild them. They are comparatively young, use the same engine family as the airline’s other A321neos, and provide useful capacity on domestic routes. Replacing them would require acquiring aircraft or reallocating existing capacity, while a structural conversion would be costly and difficult to justify for just 10 frames.

American’s larger A321neo fleet is also expanding. ch-aviation reported in February 2026 that the airline operated 75 A321-200NX aircraft alongside the 10 A321-200N jets, with additional standard-range A321neos and XLRs on order. As new aircraft arrive, the former Alaska group will represent a smaller proportion of the overall A321neo fleet, but the configuration mismatch will remain as long as those 10 aircraft continue flying.

American’s former Alaska A321neos demonstrate how aircraft standardization has limits when airlines inherit equipment designed around a different specification. The Oasis retrofit brought their interiors closer to the rest of the fleet, but no cabin refresh can relocate doors or reproduce the Cabin Flex fuselage. Their six-seat capacity gap and alternative exit-row positions are therefore likely to persist.

As American receives more A321neo aircraft, the 10 jets should become a smaller share of the overall fleet. That may reduce how frequently passengers encounter the alternate seat map, but it will not remove the need for accurate aircraft-specific information. The most useful improvement would be clearer differentiation in booking tools, allowing customers to identify the 190-seat version before paying for a particular seat. For airlines increasingly selling individual cabin positions as products, reliable seat-map accuracy is part of the service itself, not merely a back-office detail.

Destination Spotlight: Crystal Mountain

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Skiing at Crystal Mountain, Michigan. (Photo: Crystal Mountain)

As a proud Michigander, I spent many childhood summers “Up North,” a region of the state with beautiful lakes, cozy cabins, extensive hiking and biking trails, and every other manner of outdoor summer fun. But “Up North” isn’t strictly a summer destination; it’s a four-season adventure hub that offers everything from spectacular fall leaf-peeping to world-class winter skiing.

Among the best of these Michigan winter playgrounds is Crystal Mountain. Just a short drive from Traverse City and a stone’s throw from Sleeping Bear Dunes National Lakeshore, Crystal Mountain got its start 70 years ago with a single rope tow, an old car engine, and a dream. From these humble beginnings, it has blossomed into one of the best and most visited ski hills in the state, with 59 downhill runs, six chairlifts, two magic carpets, and more than 100 acres of skiable terrain.

The mission at the heart of the operation has always remained the same: to be a place where families come together to have fun, make memories, and experience all the best the outdoors have to offer. This philosophy has immortalized Crystal Mountain in the hearts of midwesterners like me, encouraging generation after generation of skiers to fall in love with the sport and the outdoors. It’s where I learned to ski—and where you can still take lessons from PSIA/AASI-trained instructors.

Beyond the ski hill, there’s no shortage of things to do at Crystal. Glide through this winter wonderland on cross-country skis—the resort’s 25 kilometers of trails were named best in the state by Midwest Living Magazine. Or let a team of Belgian horses do the gliding for you as you enjoy the views of the forests and hills from a horse-drawn carriage. Lace up your ice skates in the heart of the resort’s village at Barr Park, or explore the Michigan Legacy Art Park, a 30-acre reserve located on the grounds of Crystal Mountain where you can snowshoe, hike, and take in the sculptures and scenery.

When you’ve had enough of the cold, unwind at the outdoor hot tub, the indoor pool at the Peak Fitness Center, or the award-winning Crystal Spa. Consistently recognized as one of the best in the country, Crystal Spa features infrared saunas, eucalyptus steam rooms, and a relaxation lounge with a cozy fireplace, making it the perfect oasis after a day spent out in the winter weather.

There are endless ways to enjoy Crystal Mountain, but the true magic of this place can’t be credited to any one thing—it’s the feeling you get when you’re here. It’s the memories you’ll cherish for a lifetime.

Established in 1956, Crystal Mountain is a family-owned, four-season resort located 28 miles southwest of Traverse City, Michigan, and a short drive from Sleeping Bear Dunes National Lakeshore. The 1,500-acre resort features lodging, dining, downhill and cross-country skiing, two championship golf courses, shopping, the award-winning Crystal Spa, Michigan’s only alpine slide, Peak Fitness, the Park at Water’s Edge, Michigan Legacy Art Park, weddings, a conference center, and real estate options.

Are You Waiting for Life to Begin?

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 8, 2026 | Reviewed by Lybi Ma

What if the person who brings you to life is also the one who brings you pain?

I was thinking about my own life and how, sometimes, it can feel as though it is divided into two halves: the life we live and the life we imagine sharing with a particular person.

When I tried to picture it, I saw a page divided by a crooked line (like the line in a broken-heart emoji). Another line runs alongside it, following its shape, inseparable from it. It was the connection, even when the two people were no longer together.

If you look at this picture, you might see different things. But I see three beings. Two who could not be more different, yet somehow belong to each other. And a third one: The space between them. The grey that separates their worlds, yet also connects them. That line is a boundary and a bridge.

Sometimes, light passes through that grey and reaches the other side, turning it bright for a moment. Life becomes colorful again. There is love, warmth, and a feeling that the world might be beautiful. But when the light fades, they return to their separate worlds, to the lives they have to live apart.

Maybe it is that space between the two moments, the third part of the picture, where you find yourself suspended in midair, unable to move forward or backward, not knowing where you stand.

One becomes the freedom. The other becomes the weight that gives that freedom meaning. But perhaps neither is as free as they seem. One does not know how to stop giving. The other does not know how to stop holding on. That is why some connections are so difficult to explain: they bring out parts of us we rarely see, beautiful, yet painful.

But what if the grey world in this picture is not only about the space between two people? What if it is also about the way we live?

Think about how much of our lives we spend waiting. Waiting for the right person, the right opportunity, the right moment. We look at our present lives and see only the grey. We imagine that once someone arrives, once we finally get what we have been longing for, we will begin to live.

But while we wait for that moment, what happens to the life we are already living?

We begin to believe that life is waiting for us somewhere in the future, beyond that line. This is why some connections are so powerful. For a moment, they remind us that we can feel something we thought we had lost. Something so human that, for a second, the rest of the world vanishes. We think we miss the person, but we miss the version of ourselves who felt that alive in their presence.

Have you ever considered that the person who awakened something inside you might not be the only place where that part of you can exist? What if the freedom, the love were yours all along, even before you met them? Maybe they helped you discover a part of yourself you never knew existed.

The connection was real. What you felt was real. It could have been painful, beautiful, and unlike anything you had experienced before. None of that becomes less meaningful just because the connection ended. But why do we have to hold on to the person to keep what they helped us discover?

Maybe it is possible to let someone go without having to erase what happened. Keep the part of yourself that came alive. You do not have to return to the connection to feel those things again. You can carry them into the rest of your life.

I know that looking inside yourself isn't comfortable. It means accepting some things, questioning others, and making sense of your own feelings. It also means recognizing that a new person is not responsible for the pain left by someone who came before them.

I would tell my younger self that it is okay not to have everything figured out. It is okay to take your time. And it is okay to miss someone without going back to them.

And if you are living in the grey right now, remember that the part of you that can see the light is still there. You may have stopped looking for it. Sometimes, all you need is to believe that the other side exists. But life on the grey side can be beautiful, too, if you allow yourself to see it. An ordinary morning can be warmth, joy in a small moment, and beauty in places you might have overlooked. And when you finally see it, let yourself feel it fully.

You might ask, what is the point of all this? Well, first of all, thank you for reading almost to the end. But if I had to leave you with just one thought, it's this: no matter where you are, whom you are with, or what circumstances you are living through, your life is still yours. You may not always have a choice about what happens, but you have some choice in how you meet it. You can spend your days holding on to what hurts, waiting for life to be different, or you can slowly begin noticing the beautiful things that are already here.

There is life everywhere, even in the dirt. And no, that doesn't mean we have to pretend everything is fine or feel grateful for every difficult thing that happens. It means that even in an imperfect life, there is still something worth noticing.

Your light is always there, with you. You do not have to spend your life waiting for someone else to turn it on.
